#5232. Dogecoin Contributor Flags Social Signing as Potential Wallet Security Risk

Disclaimer: The opinions expressed by our writers are their own and do not represent the views of U.Today. The financial and market information provided on U.Today is intended for informational purposes only. U.Today is not liable for any financial losses incurred while trading cryptocurrencies. Conduct your own research by contacting financial experts before making any investment decisions. We believe that all content is accurate as of the date of publication, but certain offers mentioned may no longer be available.

Dogecoin community member Mishaboar has raised concerns about the security risks associated with social signing, a feature that allows users to create and access a blockchain wallet using everyday accounts like Google, Apple ID, Telegram, or email instead of managing a complex seed phrase.

This comes after Keplr, a multichain wallet supporting Cosmos, Bitcoin, Ethereum, and Starknet, stated it had received reports of unauthorized transactions on some Keplr accounts created with Google or Apple login.

The wallet provider stated that the incident has been escalated to the Web3Auth team, with both working together to investigate the matter.

Before Keplr's disclosure, Web3 wallet infrastructure provider Web3Auth said it was aware of a security issue affecting Web3Auth's services used by some third parties. It stated that it had temporarily paused the embedded wallet service in response to the issue; however, after an initial investigation, no evidence was found that MetaMask users were impacted.

In his X post, Mishaboar criticized social signing. "Social signing, the feature you may want to add to your wallet if you hate your users," Mishaboar wrote.

Social signing, the feature you may want to add to your wallet if you hate your users. The worst part is that the users that use the social signing option -and which will end up being hacked through it - are also often newbies, entering crypto for the first time. And the risk goes beyond the funds in your wallet - data leaks could link your real life identity to your holdings, which can have terrible consequences.

The concern is particularly significant for inexperienced crypto users who may not recognize the risks associated with connecting their personal accounts to wallet services. While the feature makes wallet creation and access easier, it could introduce security vulnerabilities that users may not fully understand, Mishaboar argued.

"The worst part is that the users that use the social signing option—and who will end up being hacked through it—are also often newbies, entering crypto for the first time. And the risk goes beyond the funds in your wallet—data leaks could link your real-life identity to your holdings, which can have terrible consequences," Mishaboar stated.

Mishaboar noted that this warning also extends to wallets using social sign-in, which allows users to back up their keys in the "Cloud," which just means somebody else's server, he explained.

This week, a security advisory was issued for iOS users, who are urged to update their devices to enhance their wallet security. This follows security researchers identifying two exploit kits, Coruna and DarkSword, which target devices running older iOS versions. These do not rely on a flaw in any wallet app, but once a device is compromised, they can access wallet data stored on it.

#5230. Democrats to Push Broader Crypto Market Structure Bill After Sweeping US Midterms: Report

Democrats to Push Broader Crypto Market Structure Bill After Sweeping US Midterms: Report

House Democrats are reportedly set to progress crypto legislation after sweeping the U.S. midterms.

There are plans to expand the Crypto Investor Consumer Bill of Rights—a Clarity Act amendment led by Dem. Maxine Waters—into a broader market structure bill, journalist Eleanor Terrett tweeted. The proposal would include giving the SEC more power to supervise the crypto markets and limiting the role of the CFTC, sources told Terrett.

🚨SCOOP: House Democrats are discussing plans to expand the Crypto Investor and Consumer Bill of Rights, @RepMaxineWaters’ amendment to the Clarity Act in 2025, into a broader crypto “market structure” bill next Congress.

The proposal would fold in Democratic Clarity Act…

— Eleanor Terrett (@EleanorTerrett) November 4, 2026

On the surface, the midterm election results could be seen as a major hit to crypto policy in the U.S., with President Trump's Republican administration widely regarded as the pro-crypto party, while the Democrats were deemed more hostile. However, experts, told Decrypt, the tide might be turning and crypto policy evolving into a more bipartisan issue.

Advocacy organization Stand With Crypto endorsed 45 candidates in the midterms, per its election tracker, with 27 pro-crypto candidate wins, 12 losses, and six races still live at the time of writing. Nearly half of the endorsed candidates were Democrats.

"We’re proud to see a majority of our endorsed candidates win their elections," Mason Lynaugh, executive director of Stand With Crypto, said in a statement shared with Decrypt. "To the candidates who won’t return to Washington, thank you for going to bat for crypto-owning Americans when it counted."

Congratulations to the pro-crypto champions who won their elections, including more than 50 pro-crypto new members heading to Congress.

In the closest races, our advocates showed up: in Florida's 9th District, Darren Soto won by 1,477 votes. In that same district, we mobilized… pic.twitter.com/lcq055GNET

— Stand With Crypto🛡️ (@standwithcrypto) November 4, 2026

James Butterfill, head of research at CoinShares, described Republican Bryan Steil as a "meaningful loss" for crypto. The Wisconsin Congressman was the chair of the House digital assets subcommittee and closely involved in efforts to advance the Clarity Act—which were stymied when it stalled in the Senate earlier this year.

That said, Butterfill also highlighted several winners—Democrats Chris Pappas in New Hampshire and James Talarico in Texas, as well as Wyoming Republican Harriet Hageman—who have supported crypto policy. He stressed, however, that the challenge is now "converting" that support into passing legislation. Some believe that could slow an already difficult path for the Clarity Act.

"I wouldn’t say it’s game over for Clarity, but passing it has become tougher," Carlos Guzman, vice president of research at crypto trading firm GSR, told Decrypt. "Some Democrats support clearer crypto rules, but I wouldn’t expect it to be high on their agenda. Any agreement will likely require stronger provisions on things like consumer protection and conflicts of interest."

The Clarity Act looks to establish rules for crypto markets and has been batted back and forth since 2025. Democrats have pushed back on the bill, citing Trump's crypto conflicts of interest, with Senator Elizabeth Warren (D-MA) pushing for the bill to ban officials and their families from profiting from the sector.

"For other crypto policies, the election doesn’t automatically replace the leadership of the SEC or CFTC or stop their rulemaking," Guzman said. "Congressional oversight does change, however, so we might see more scrutiny of regulatory decisions, with Democrats also able to influence agency funding and future appointments."

Butterfill added that he believes crypto is becoming less dependent on the Clarity Act, as the SEC and CFTC push for regulatory clarity without the need for Congress. He also said that AI regulation may take eyes away from crypto, slowing crypto policy in the process.

Following the midterms, Bitcoin has exhibited volatility, and is currently trading at around $76,820, down 4.1% on the day, per Decrypt data.

"The result alone would not make me change my medium-term Bitcoin outlook," Butterfill told Decrypt. "Slower reform could delay some institutional activity, but we would expect the more direct impact to fall on US crypto businesses reliant on new regulatory permissions. We would revisit the outlook if political uncertainty materially weakened investment flows or tightened financial conditions."

#5229. US Adds 165,000 Jobs in October: Why Bitcoin Traders Are Watching the Fed

Strict editorial policy that focuses on accuracy, relevance, and impartiality

Morbi pretium leo et nisl aliquam mollis. Quisque arcu lorem, ultricies quis pellentesque nec, ullamcorper eu odio.

TL;DR: The US economy added 165,000 payroll jobs in October while unemployment stood at 4.1%, according to the Bureau of Labor Statistics’ November 6 release. The data complicate hopes of quick monetary easing, but the report by itself cannot establish what the Federal Reserve will do next or where Bitcoin will trade.

Bitcoin’s difficult trading week has coincided with a closely watched update on the US economy. The Bureau of Labor Statistics reported on November 6 that nonfarm payroll employment increased by 165,000 in October. The unemployment rate was little changed at 4.1%.

For cryptocurrency traders, the significance is not that payrolls have a mechanical relationship with Bitcoin’s price. Rather, employment data are one part of the evidence the Federal Reserve weighs when deciding whether inflation and economic activity justify changing interest rates.

The BLS said employment increased in professional and business services, healthcare, and transportation and warehousing. Professional and business services added 70,000 jobs, substantially above that category’s recent monthly average. The report also revised August and September gains upward by a combined 84,000 jobs.

Those revisions matter because monthly payroll estimates are not fixed snapshots of economic reality. More complete information can materially alter the picture after the first publication. Anyone building a large market argument on a single month should therefore examine the trend and the revisions, not only the latest number.

Average hourly earnings for private-sector employees rose by 0.4% in October and 3.1% over 12 months, according to the same release. Wage developments can affect the inflation outlook, although inflation depends on many other factors including productivity, goods costs and service-sector conditions.

When investors expect a lower path for policy rates, the relative attraction of cash and short-duration interest-bearing assets may fall, potentially supporting risk assets. A stronger labour market can complicate that narrative by giving policymakers less reason to ease quickly. But the Fed’s response depends on the whole data set, not an automatic rule that more jobs always mean higher rates.

Bitcoin is not a conventional corporate security with earnings that can be discounted using a single valuation model. Even so, it trades within financial markets where leverage costs, dollar liquidity and investor risk appetite change with the broader macroeconomic environment. That connection can become particularly visible during sharp stock-market moves.

There is also a separate institutional channel. NewsBTC reported that spot Bitcoin ETFs suffered $398.6 million in net withdrawals on November 2, reflecting flows into and out of listed investment products. Those fund numbers are useful but do not show why each investor traded.

Market commentary has linked recent cryptocurrency weakness to technology-stock volatility and changing interest-rate expectations. That can be a plausible interpretation of contemporaneous movements, but correlation is not proof of one exclusive cause. Leverage, derivatives positioning and crypto-specific events may be operating at the same time.

The important next readings include inflation releases, future employment revisions and Federal Reserve communications. Traders should also follow the distinction between network fundamentals and investment-market behaviour explored in NewsBTC’s hashrate coverage. A shift in financial conditions can affect market prices long before it changes the underlying protocol.

For now, October’s jobs report provides a firmer data point in the interest-rate debate. It does not supply a confident Bitcoin price target or a guaranteed forecast for the next Fed decision.

Can Bitcoin climb back to $82,000? A recent forecast says BTC must reclaim that level before any real recovery, even as cumulative spot Bitcoin ETF inflows slip to about $56 billion.

That makes the next few weeks pivotal for any Bitcoin price prediction.

BTC trades in a narrow range well below its record high, and a possible Fed rate cut is the catalyst traders now watch.

Traders who position for that move also keep an eye on early-stage projects.

One of them is MEMASA ($MEMASA), an upcoming AI trading app that lets users research and trade crypto through a chat.

Bitcoin has drifted lower for a month, and a recent 24/7 Wall St analysis says the key test now sits near $82,000.BTC needs a sustained close above that level, the analysis argues, before any real recovery can take hold.

The main reason is weak fund flows, as cumulative spot Bitcoin ETF inflows have slipped to about $56 billion. Only a couple of weeks earlier, that total was higher, which means large investors have been taking money out rather than adding.

Monetary policy offers little help either, since the Fed’s benchmark rate recently moved up instead of down. Still, the analysis sees a return above $100,000 as plausible within the next couple of years, if ETF demand and Fed cuts line up.

Bitcoin now trades near $76,000, down almost 9% over the past month after a steady slide.

MEMASA is an upcoming AI crypto trading app that lets users analyze markets, check token risks, and trade on-chain by chatting with an AI assistant.

The token’s potential derives from stronger sentiment across the crypto market and growing demand within the MEMASA ecosystem. If the app attracts a wide audience, it might drive the demand for $MEMASA.

MEMASA is currently offering early access to $MEMASA through an on-chain early sale, with the present stage set at $0.0155, below the project’s anticipated public listing price. Earlier stages offer lower prices, while later stages provide faster token unlocks.

A Bitcoin rally could create favorable conditions for MEMASA’s debut. The bigger test will be whether the app delivers on its promise to simplify crypto trading and attracts enough users to build lasting demand for its token.

Bitcoin’s next move hinges on whether buyers defend support near $73,500. A push back above $82,000, with ETF inflows returning, would give bulls their first real win.

BTC has still more than doubled over three years, so this slump follows a long climb. A Fed rate cut remains the catalyst to watch.

MEMASA faces a different test, as it needs to turn an AI trading concept into an app people use regularly. If it succeeds, demand for its utility token could grow with the ecosystem. Both Bitcoin’s path and MEMASA’s outcome remain uncertain, and higher potential comes with higher risk.

#5226. Solana Labs’ Leap Launches Mobile App for Trading Tokenized Stocks and Crypto

Solana Labs’ Leap Launches Mobile App for Trading Tokenized Stocks and Crypto

Solana Labs is betting the network’s next wave of traders wants more than meme coins.

Leap, a new company under Solana Labs, launched a beta of its mobile trading app Thursday, letting users trade tokenized stocks, ETFs, metals, currencies, and crypto in one place, around the clock and subject to restrictions.

The app is available in early access on iPhone through Apple’s public TestFlight program, with an Android version coming soon. It isn’t available in the U.K. or other restricted regions.

Leap is built by the team behind Dialect, which Solana Labs acquired earlier this year. Dialect developed Solana Actions and Blinks, a protocol that turns blockchain transactions into shareable links, which launched in 2024, as well as the cashtags feature on X.

The company is pitching the app as an alternative to fast-paced meme coin trading, aimed at people who want to make better-informed decisions over the long term. Its research team curates market-moving news and pins each story to the price chart at the moment it broke.

The app tracks profit and loss across every deposit, withdrawal, trade and fee, including each position’s breakeven price. Users enter an amount in dollars, and each trade executes as an onchain swap.

Leap uses Jupiter for trade routing, Helius for infrastructure and Birdeye for market data.

“The Solana blockchain now confirms a trade in about 150 milliseconds thanks to Alpenglow, so the network is now faster than ever,” said Chris Osborn, Leap’s general manager and Dialect’s founder. Alpenglow a consensus overhaul designed to sharply cut Solana’s confirmation times.

“Every app that brings new people onchain makes the whole ecosystem stronger,” said Solana Labs co-founder and CEO Anatoly Yakovenko.

Leap isn’t charging its own fees at launch, though network and routing costs apply. It plans to use future fees to buy and burn SOL, permanently removing tokens from circulation, and to fund a referral program. Early waitlist members, dubbed the Founders Club, would earn 30% of the trading fees generated by users they invite and 5% from those users’ own referrals.

Leap arrives as tokenized stocks gain traction on Solana and beyond. Securitize has brought tokenized Nvidia, Apple, and Amazon shares to Solana. The trend has also met pushback: 56 companies have objected to having their shares traded on OKX and ICE’s planned tokenized stock venue.

#5225. Argentine Bitcoiners Debut “The Genesis of Satoshi Nakamoto” Artwork in Downtown Buenos Aires

“The Genesis of Satoshi Nakamoto,” a 2.4-meter, multi-dimensional cube, was presented on October 29 at Polo Dot as part of the Open Fest of LABITCONF 2026. Driven by Bitcoin.ar through the Premio B·Arte, the work combines art, technology, and education in a journey through Bitcoin’s fundamental principles. The inauguration featured guided remarks by Rodolfo Andragnes and a musical performance by Mayumaná, according to an announcement by the organization.

Buenos Aires now has a monument dedicated to Satoshi Nakamoto, the person—or group of people—behind the creation of Bitcoin, whose identity remains unknown.

Driven by Bitcoin.ar through the fifth edition of the Premio B·Arte, the monument was conceived not only as a tribute to Satoshi, but also as an artistic and educational experience. Through its five faces—four lateral and one upper—it proposes a journey to approach different concepts that explain Bitcoin’s origin, functioning, and philosophy.

During the inauguration, Rodolfo Andragnes — co-founder of Bitcoin.ar and LABITCONF — accompanied the public with words and explanations about the details of the work, guiding the reading of its various elements and the meaning behind each representation. The presentation was accompanied by a musical show by Mayumaná, which added rhythm and sound to the opening of this new space dedicated to Bitcoin’s history.

Each of the monument’s faces offers a gateway to a different aspect of Bitcoin. The figure of Satoshi Nakamoto, for example, represents the anonymous character of its creator and the collective dimension of a technology that, once set in motion, continued to grow thanks to thousands of people distributed around the world. The reflections and multiplied geometric forms also evoke the structure of a network that replicates and expands.

Another of its faces addresses Bitcoin’s programmed scarcity through a cube that successively divides into halves in a Fibonacci sequence. The representation refers to “the halving”, the mechanism that approximately every four years halves the reward miners receive for validating blocks and that regulates the issuance of new bitcoins. Starting at 50 bitcoins per block, the total reward gets cut in half every four years for roughly 130 years, until 21 million bitcoins have been emitted and the block reward becomes zero. This supply limit is iconic to the cryptocurrency and key to its qualities as hard money.

The work also incorporates the first words of Bitcoin’s foundational document, the white paper published by Satoshi Nakamoto in October 31st,  2008. There, the possibility of making electronic payments directly between people, without needing to go through a financial institution, is proposed. That idea constitutes a foundational principle of the technology.

Another face reproduces information from the genesis block, the first block of the Bitcoin chain, created on January 3, 2009. Among its elements appears the reference to the headline from The Times newspaper that Satoshi incorporated into that block, a historical mark that places Bitcoin’s birth in its context and anchors it to history and time.

Thus, the monument proposes something more than contemplating a sculpture: it invites one to walk around it, observe it from different angles, and discover the concepts behind each of its parts; “The intention is to bring Bitcoin closer to those who are already part of its community, but also to awaken the curiosity of those who do not yet know its history.”

The monument was created by Ricardo Alonso, engineer, and Celeste Difabio Videla, architect, a couple of artists from Bariloche who won the fifth edition of the Premio B·Arte. The work measures 2.40 meters in width, length, and height, and is made of stainless steel and glass.

Its construction involved teams and collaborators from different localities across the country, in a process that combined different trades, materials, and techniques. Aída Pippo, visual artist, researcher of new technologies applied to art, and member of the Board of Directors of Bitcoin.ar, led the project and coordinated the different stages of production and assembly.

“It gives me great pride to feel that this is something we can leave in our city and in our country,” Andragnes expressed during the presentation, highlighting the value of a collective initiative that seeks to leave a cultural and educational footprint.

For their part, the creators noted that they sought for the work to function as a meeting point for the community and, at the same time, as a gateway for those who do not yet know Bitcoin. “You don’t always have the possibility of leaving something for posterity,” they shared when speaking of the experience of seeing the monument finished and installed in Buenos Aires.

With this inauguration, Bitcoin.ar seeks for the monument to become a permanent resource for the city: a space that schools, universities, families, and visitors can approach to learn Bitcoin’s history, discover its principles, and open new conversations about the future of money and technology. The intention is that what is presented today as an artistic work can continue to grow through educational activities and new experiences, and remain in Buenos Aires as a legacy for future generations.

The project was financed by the Fundación Bitcoin Iberoamérica and represents a new way of bringing Bitcoin’s history and principles to public and cultural spaces, beyond screens and specialized environments.

#5224. EIP-8037 state-gas rules force Ethereum dApps to rewrite billing

A Nov. 6 appeal asks projects to test gas assumptions and report repair options that could shape Glamsterdam’s specifications.

Ethereum’s core developers are asking application teams to help decide whether proposed gas changes in the Glamsterdam upgrade need revision, because their own audits cannot cover every project’s contracts and off-chain services.

In a Nov. 6 post on the Ethereum Foundation blog, Marius van der Wijden asked developers and auditors to check eight gas assumptions affected by the proposed upgrade. Security auditors ChainSecurity and Dedaub are investigating alongside internal audits, but the review also needs projects to explain how they could repair affected systems.

The Hoodi testnet’s planned Oct. 26 activation and accompanying 200 million gas-limit increase were postponed on Oct. 22.

The Nov. 6 appeal says the delay continues pending resolution, while the notices leave mainnet activation unset.

The consequences extend from relayers recovering too little of their transaction costs to wallet authorizations becoming reusable after a particular kind of failure. That makes application repairability part of the evidence developers need when weighing changes to the upgrade.

Glamsterdam’s EIP-8037 would raise the cost of creating persistent blockchain data and meter that state growth separately from execution, and EIP-8038 would reprice access and writes to existing state.

The aim is to support higher throughput while controlling the data Ethereum nodes must retain.

The eight-invariant framework separates three price assumptions, covering fixed budgets and reserves, from five accounting assumptions involving gas returns, failure handling, billing and delegation.

A relayer pays to execute a transaction for someone else and may recover that expense by measuring gas before and after the work. The framework’s INV-5 entry explains how the proposed accounting can disrupt that calculation.

Under EIP-8037, a transaction initially funds a separate state-gas reservoir when its gas limit exceeds 16,777,216. State creation draws from that pool first, then execution gas. Solidity’s gasleft() reading excludes the reservoir, so subtracting two readings can miss costs that the relayer actually pays.

If the later reading exceeds the earlier one, checked subtraction reverts, blocking the operation. Unchecked subtraction can wrap into a large number and potentially produce excessive reimbursement, depending on the surrounding arithmetic and payment checks.

Meanwhile, invisible reservoir spending can leave users undercharged and operators covering the shortfall.

The billing guidance suggests agreeing on a user-signed fee using an estimate under the new rules. Where metering remains necessary, clamping the subtraction and capping payment at a signed maximum limits the charge, while reservoir-funded costs remain outside the measurement.

An open specification proposal, PR #12581, would prevent gas readings from increasing. That would address the subtraction problem, but the reservoir’s billing blind spot would remain.

Under the proposed Glamsterdam rules, gasleft() excludes the state-gas reservoir. Select a funding state to see how the balance charged for one cold new-slot write changes.

Illustrative state with at least 97,920 gas in the reservoir at this write. The execution balance pays 12,100 and the reservoir pays 97,920.

Illustrative state with zero reservoir gas at this write. The execution balance pays the complete 110,020 charge, including the state component.

A funded reservoir can pay the state charge outside gasleft(), while an empty reservoir shifts that charge to the execution balance.

Proposed rules checked November 6, 2026. Operation-level charges exclude intrinsic, wrapper and incidental instruction costs. Initial reservoir funding requires a transaction gas limit above 16,777,216, and later refills may also fund it. Intermediate reservoir balances split the state charge between balances.

Uses the proposed charge for one cold zero-to-nonzero storage write. 12,100 execution + 97,920 state = 110,020 total gas. Funding illustrations assume at least 97,920 reservoir gas or zero at that write. The main bar is proportional, rounded to 0.001 SVG units. Connectors are schematic.

Proposed rules checked November 6, 2026. Operation-level charges exclude intrinsic, wrapper and incidental instruction costs. Initial reservoir funding requires a transaction gas limit above 16,777,216, and later refills may also fund it. Intermediate reservoir balances split the state charge between balances.

INV-8 examines EIP-7702 delegation, where an account authorizes delegated contract code. Under the proposed rules, insufficient gas during pre-execution can leave a transaction included in a block while rolling back its delegation and authorization-related update to the account’s sequence number, or nonce.

An exposed authorization that still satisfies its validity checks, including the authorizing account’s nonce, can then be reused. If the delegated contract also has an initializer that lets the first caller choose the owner, an attacker could apply the authorization and initialize ownership in one transaction.

Authorization front-running already existed in public mempools, but here a failed mined transaction can expose a reusable authorization even after private submission.

Delegations already applied before the top-level execution frame remain in place when that later frame fails. A failed receipt requires further checking of the account’s resulting code and nonce.

Suggested wallet safeguards include binding initialization to the account’s signature or self-calls and invalidating exposed authorizations. Charging the worst-case authorization cost upfront has also been suggested as a protocol change and remains unadopted.

Under the proposed schedule, creating a cold storage slot costs 110,020 total gas, versus 22,100 under Osaka. Of that proposed total, 12,100 is execution gas, and 97,920 is state gas, with the reservoir determining which balance funds the latter.

ChainSecurity’s Oct. 13 research shows how changed costs and returned gas can let a contract finish recording an underfunded operation as permanently unsuccessful. If that record removes the retry path, a valid operation can become impossible to repeat.

Revalidated budgets, propagating failures, and preserving retries address different parts of that risk.

The repository asks affected projects whether a setting, upgrade, or migration can fix their problem, and roughly when. Those answers help developers assess how much compatibility work applications can absorb and where specification changes could prevent damage.

Concrete reports of what breaks and how to repair it will inform the next specification discussions.

#5223. Millions watched Kalshi’s election odds without betting a dollar

On election night, millions watched Kalshi's probabilities shift in real time, even though most visitors never traded a contract.

Kalshi says people watching its midterm markets outnumbered traders five to one. For those spectators, Kalshi offers a forecast that keeps moving while the country waits for an answer.

You could spend election night on Kalshi without buying anything, as millions of people did this week. Its midterm display offered maps of congressional races and gauges tracking control of the House and Senate, with percentages changing in real time as the evening unfolded.

It's easy to understand why traders keep such a close eye on Kalshi: every little change in odds changes the value of their positions. Given how much money actually moved hands during the midterms, it's no wonder hundreds of thousands of traders were glued to the platforms.

But millions of people were also looking at Kalshi who weren't making any bets, with most not even having accounts on the platform. In October, Kalshi said viewers of its midterm markets outnumbered traders five to one.

Kalshi’s post-election recap reported more than $2.5 billion in midterm trading volume, compared with $1.3 billion for the 2024 election cycle.

Kalshi's Nov. 4, 2026 recap reports $1.3 billion for the 2024 election cycle and more than $2.5 billion for 2026 midterm markets. The 2026 bar marks the reported lower-bound threshold, not an exact total. Kalshi uses notional volume to measure trading activity; these figures do not count viewers or unique traders. The recap does not specify matching market coverage or start and end times for the two periods.

Source: Kalshi: 2026 midterms recap, Nov. 4, 2026 · Kalshi: prediction-market volume convention, footnote 1

While Kalshi's numbers are hard to verify and the company hasn't provided an exact measurement period or a definition of its ratio, they show a second use for the exchange: following politics without placing a bet, while letting the people who do bet help shape your expectations.

On Aug. 20, 2008, Wolf Blitzer turned to CNN's internet reporter, Abbi Tatton, for an update on speculation about Barack Obama's running mate. Tatton brought him a number from Intrade, an online prediction market: Joe Biden was trading at about 36%.

Biden led the other candidates she named and would ultimately get the job, although 36% still left substantially more room for someone else to be chosen.

Tatton also mentioned CNN's own political market, which used virtual dollars. Viewers could follow the odds or play along without risking real money. Everybody wanted to put a number on political suspense, and it was a perfect fit for a television segment about the elections.

Thanks for signing up to The Catalyst. Look out for your first daily email. If you already receive it, you’re all set.

Kalshi's election hub combines market odds with polling averages, fundraising figures, and campaign news, giving visitors several ways to follow a race in one place.

A contract paying $1 if a candidate wins, and nothing if they lose, might trade at 65 cents. That price is commonly read as roughly a 65% chance of victory, although fees, available trading volume, and the contract's rules slightly erode those numbers.

Polls gather answers from a sample of people, forecasts estimate an outcome based on historical evidence and assumptions, and a market then sets a price at which participants will trade. These can inform one another, but a percentage alone doesn't reveal how it was produced or what it measures.

The trader has to decide whether 65 cents is worth paying, and the spectator can simply take away the expectation that the candidate will probably win. That means that someone else's financial judgment has become part of their understanding of the election.

The market still has something to show you between a poll's release and the next substantial development. It can adjust after a debate, absorb new returns, or reflect a change in how traders interpret already available evidence.

Those movements look much the same on a screen: a percentage rises or falls, inviting the viewer to read the change as news.

Kalshi acknowledges in its explanation of why prices move that fresh information is only one cause. Trader behavior can also push prices away from a reasonable estimate, and watching the percentage doesn't necessarily reveal what has happened.

A candidate can appear to be gaining ground because the chance attached to their name has increased, even when no additional voter has been interviewed and no additional ballot counted.

An experiment involving 1,994 registered voters showed how easily those percentages can shape expectations.

Participants saw pairs of hypothetical congressional candidates, with randomly varied polling figures and market odds. Higher displayed odds made respondents more likely to identify a candidate as the expected winner.

Traders can bring knowledge that a casual observer lacks, and money gives someone who spots a mistaken price a reason to act. The commercial effort to attract an audience doesn't remove the possibility that the resulting forecast is good.

But a poll and a market reacting to that poll can look like two separate endorsements of a candidate's prospects when they draw on the same evidence. Those just observing the market get more perspectives without necessarily getting more independent information.

In its post-election recap, Kalshi said its markets reached 99% for Democratic House control at 8:10 p.m. Eastern and passed that level for Senate control at 9 p.m.

The company said its prices reflected anticipated results ahead of television networks and offered replays showing how the graphics changed through the evening.

But an exchange and a newsroom have to answer different questions, since a market can express overwhelming confidence while leaving room for a loss.

The Associated Press says it calls a race only when the trailing candidates no longer have a path to victory. For congressional control, it counts the seats actually needed for a majority.

Neither a price nor a news organization's call is the official certification of an election. But the call takes responsibility for saying the outcome has been established, a different claim from assigning it a probability.

A market that gets to 99% first doesn't show that journalists took longer to perform the same task.

AP supplies Kalshi with vote counts and race calls, which the exchange places alongside its forecasts and traders can use to reconsider their positions. The market depends in part on the reporting against which it is judged.

Kalshi's recap points readers toward international elections and the 2028 presidential contest, inviting them to follow the odds beyond one election night.

Spectators will most likely want an early estimate from a prediction market but still wait for a newsroom to declare a winner.

The price of these contracts shows how strongly traders expect an outcome, and the race call says the remaining votes can no longer change it. People who never risk a dollar still have to decide how much authority to give the price, especially when its movement is the only thing that has changed.

Armed with a classical education and an eye for news, Andjela dove head deep into the crypto industry in 2018 after spending years covering politics.

#5222. South Korean Crypto Exchange Gopax Suspends Withdrawals and Trading After Hacking Threat

South Korea's Gopax halted standard withdrawals at 2:55 p.m. and trading at 3:53 p.m. Friday after a hacking-threat email, and is transferring all held assets to cold wallets with no loss reported so far.

South Korean cryptocurrency exchange Gopax suspended withdrawals and trading on Friday after receiving a hacking-threat email, and said it is moving all held virtual assets into offline cold wallets as a preemptive step to protect customer funds.

Gopax halted standard deposit and withdrawal services at 2:55 p.m. local time and stopped trading at 3:53 p.m., Yonhap News Agency reported, citing the exchange. Direct withdrawals through the website and app are unavailable. Customers who want coins out must apply through the customer support center, where requests are processed manually after internal verification.

“To ensure the safe custody of user assets, we plan to transfer all virtual assets to cold wallets for storage,” Gopax said in remarks carried by Seoul Economic Daily. “Direct withdrawals through the Gopax website or app are temporarily unavailable, and customers wishing to withdraw funds should apply through the Gopax customer support center.”

No actual loss of assets has been reported. The company has not set a date to reopen trading or standard withdrawals.

With trading offline, all open orders were canceled, Chosun Biz reported. Deposits were initially paused with withdrawals; later Korean coverage said deposits had been restored while web and app withdrawals stayed shut. Manual withdrawals through support will take longer than usual.

A cold wallet is a storage setup kept offline, cut off from the internet that hot wallets use to process customer sends. Gopax said the shift of remaining hot-wallet balances into cold storage is meant to shrink the surface available to anyone who can reach online systems.

A Gopax official told Yonhap the email came from a party claiming it could hack the exchange, but that the threat itself had not been verified. “We ask for your understanding, as this measure is intended to prevent financial incidents and protect user assets,” the company said.

The freeze lands in a week when exchange security is already under a bright light. On Oct. 29, attackers drained about $6.34 billion from Binance hot wallets in what Binance later described as a months-long campaign that began with a fake job interview. The FBI has tied that theft to North Korea; Binance has reopened withdrawals in stages and said it is covering customer losses from its own funds.

Brazil’s Mercado Bitcoin froze deposits and withdrawals earlier this week after its own security incident, with preliminary estimates of suspicious transfers near $40 million. Gopax’s case is different on the facts disclosed so far: the exchange is describing a threat email and a precautionary lockdown, not a confirmed drain.

Gopax itself has been dealing with account trouble. From early September, abnormal access hit some user accounts, leaking personal information on seven customers and two current or former employees, with further leakage confirmed last month, Yonhap’s Korean dispatch said. The company has treated that breach as separate from Friday’s threat email; any link has not been confirmed.

South Korea’s Financial Services Commission has also been pressing domestic platforms after a broader wave of cybersecurity incidents across financial firms. Korean exchanges are already required to keep at least 80% of customer crypto in cold storage. Friday’s move pushes Gopax further offline while it checks whether the threat is real.

For bitcoin holders on Gopax, the practical effect is a queue: coins are being parked offline while support staff approve outbound sends one by one. That is slower than an app button, and it is the trade the exchange says it is making to keep balances intact.

The test for Gopax now is whether it can finish the cold-wallet transfer, clear the manual withdrawal queue without a loss, and say when trading comes back — or whether the unverified threat turns into the incident it was trying to get ahead of.

#5221. Hollywood Studios Quietly Ban Staff From Betting on Prediction Markets: Report

Hollywood Studios Quietly Ban Staff From Betting on Prediction Markets: Report

Hollywood studios and production companies have been quietly issuing guidelines barring employees from betting on prediction markets, entertainment industry trade paper The Hollywood Reporter reported on Thursday.

The warnings prohibit staff from betting on events such as studio earnings, casting decisions and release dates, or passing information to friends and family so they can bet instead, according to the report. At least one company has reportedly set up a hotline for employees to anonymously report colleagues suspected of placing bets.

The Hollywood Reporter's own parent company, Penske Media, has also updated its employee handbook in recent weeks to ban bets on "award nominations, casting decisions, production timelines and release dates," the outlet said.

The rules extend long-standing bans on insider trading in the stock market to platforms such as Kalshi and Polymarket, which let users bet on everything from who will be cast as the next James Bond to who will be voted off a reality show.

"You now have, essentially, an explosion in the number of employers who have to think about insider trading rules from an ethical standpoint," Joshua Mitts, a law professor at Columbia University who studies prediction markets, told the outlet.

Enforcing those rules may be another matter. "With blockchain-based trading, it can be basically impossible if you know how to use a mixer correctly and know how to hide your identity behind multiple layers of intermediation," Mitts said.

Some bookmakers are already spooked. Betting site BetOnline temporarily pulled its market on the next James Bond this week after a sudden wave of "sharp money" pushed actor Callum Turner above longtime favorite Jack Lowden, raising concerns about a leak, according to the report.

Entertainment insiders have been caught before. In February, Kalshi said a video editor at MrBeast's company had made about $5,000 using confidential information to bet on MrBeast content; he was fired and fined $20,000.

Federal prosecutors have also brought cases over insider trading on prediction markets. In April, an active-duty U.S. soldier was charged with using classified information about the raid to capture Nicolás Maduro to bet on Polymarket. In May, a Google engineer was charged with commodities fraud, wire fraud and money laundering over Polymarket bets allegedly placed using confidential company data. Both cases are pending.

Governments have moved too. California, New York and Illinois have banned state employees from using nonpublic information to bet on prediction markets, while the platforms have rolled out their own surveillance tools. Polymarket has investigated dozens of bets on the U.S. midterm elections.

One kind of entertainment bet is already off the table. Under the Onion Futures Act of 1958, U.S. law explicitly bans just two types of futures contracts: those on onions and those on movie box office receipts, The Hollywood Reporter noted.

#5219. Morpho loan stress exposes cbXRP, but spares native XRP

A Nov. 6 snapshot placed 8.84 million USDC across 1,070 positions within a further 10% oracle decline, assuming unchanged debt and collateral.

A 10% decline from the collateral price recorded around 13:24 UTC on Nov. 6 would make positions carrying about 8.84 million USDC of debt eligible for liquidation in a Morpho lending market on Base.

The snapshot covered 1,070 positions borrowing USDC against cbXRP, Coinbase's XRP-backed token. A smaller 5% decline would bring 462 of those positions, carrying about 3.23 million USDC of debt, to or beyond the same boundary.

For XRP holders who borrow against their tokens, further price weakness creates a choice between repaying debt, committing more collateral, or facing liquidation. Borrowing preserves exposure to the asset, while adding a financial obligation that becomes harder to support as collateral loses value.

Coinbase describes cbXRP as a wrapped token backed 1:1 by native XRP held in its custody. A holder can use an existing XRP position as collateral, so rising loan balances alone do not create new XRP purchases.

At 13:24 UTC, the market carried about 59.2 million USDC of total debt against roughly 119.9 million cbXRP in collateral. The debt inside the further-10% stress bucket represented approximately 15% of that borrowing, and the affected positions held about 12.73 million cbXRP.

Conditional exposure in one Base cbXRP/USDC market, using November 6 positions with debt and collateral quantities held fixed.

An illustrative 60% starting LTV rises to 66.7% when collateral value falls 10%, leaving debt and collateral quantities unchanged.

At the same lower oracle price, repaying 10% of original debt restores the illustrative position to 60% LTV.

Adding about 11.1% to original collateral quantity at the lower oracle price restores 60% LTV.

The 5% group sits inside the 10% group. Repayments or added collateral can change the outcome.

Actual seizures and subsequent native-XRP sales require separate transactions. Borrower example is hypothetical and excludes interest, fees and further price changes.

Its liquidation loan-to-value threshold is fixed at 62.5%. As the collateral price falls, the same debt takes up a larger share of the backing, bringing the position closer to liquidation eligibility.

The market's oracle, the price feed used to value collateral, uses Chainlink's XRP/USD feed with a fixed 1:1 USDC leg, and its reported ratio was about 1.1843 USDC per cbXRP.

Borrower actions can change the exposed balance before prices reach either scenario.

Consider a position starting at 60% LTV. A 10% fall in the oracle price, with debt and collateral quantities unchanged, raises its LTV to about 66.7%, beyond this market's 62.5% threshold.

Repaying 10% of the debt would restore the original 60% LTV at the lower price. Adding about 11.1% to the original collateral quantity would achieve the same result, excluding interest, fees, and further price moves.

The market interface recorded repayments and collateral additions around 13:14-13:16 UTC on Nov. 6. Those transactions show that positions were changing around the observation window.

For borrowers using Coinbase's interface to Morpho on Base, optional automatic collateral protection can add assets after a configured trigger. Borrowers must enable it and have assets available, and volatility or technical problems can prevent execution.

That makes the fixed-position estimate sensitive to both borrower responses and whether intended top-ups actually complete.

Once a position becomes eligible, an external liquidator can repay some or all of its debt and receive collateral plus an incentive. In this market, that exchanges USDC debt repayment for cbXRP collateral.

The exposed positions hold 12.7 million cbXRP, but the amount actually seized depends on subsequent liquidation transactions.

A liquidator may retain or trade that collateral, and any resulting native-XRP sales would require separate evidence linking the liquidation to those sales.

For holders using XRP-backed credit, the immediate pressure is to keep debt supported as collateral values change. Subsequent repayments, collateral additions and executed liquidations will determine how much of this snapshot's exposure remains.

#5215. Bitcoin Price Holds Near $75,000 After Hot October Jobs Report

U.S. employers added 165,000 jobs in October, nearly three times the 59,000 economists expected, the Labor Department said Friday, and the bitcoin price briefly pushed above $76,100 before settling near $75,500, Bitcoin Magazine Pro data shows.

The unemployment rate edged down to 4.1% from 4.2%, beating the Dow Jones forecast that called for it to hold. Average hourly earnings rose 0.4% on the month and 3.1% over the year, both ahead of estimates, CNBC reported.

Prior months were revised higher as well. August payrolls moved to 186,000, an upward revision of 53,000, and September rose to 60,000 after a 31,000 upgrade, leaving the two-month total 84,000 stronger than first reported.

The print lands on a market still digesting Thursday’s Nasdaq-led rout. The bitcoin price had held near $76,000 into that close, dipped toward $75,200 late in the session, then recovered into the mid-$75,000s overnight before the 8:30 a.m. ET release. Spot bitcoin changed hands around $75,800 after the data, with a market cap of about $1.52 trillion and roughly $48 billion in trading volume over the past day, Yahoo Finance data indicated.

Bitcoin price’s first move was a quick spike above $76,100, then a slide toward $75,600 before it settled back near $75,800. The range on the day has run from about $75,240 to $76,280, leaving the market little changed from its pre-release level even as rate futures repriced.

That muted finish fits the pattern after big macro prints. Most of the damage to the bitcoin price this week came from equity risk-off, not from the labor market itself, and a hot payrolls number mainly reinforces the path the Fed was already pricing.

CME FedWatch odds put roughly a 79% chance of a 25-basis-point hike at the Dec. 9 meeting after the release, up from about 70% into the open. The next employment report is due Dec. 4, five days before that decision, and the signal points the same way the last Fed hold did: stronger hiring and firmer wages leave less room for another pause.

Thursday’s session still sets the floor under the bitcoin price. The Nasdaq Composite fell about 5.5% and dropped into correction territory, more than 10% below its recent high, while the S&P 500 lost about 4.4% and the Dow shed roughly 1,800 points, or 3.4%, CNBC reported.

The Magnificent Seven erased more than $1 trillion in market value. Nvidia alone lost more than 9%, wiping out roughly $440 billion in capitalization, as investors priced in a slower AI data-center buildout after the Democratic midterm sweep.

Bitcoin has traded with that AI-heavy complex for most of the year, so a correction in the Nasdaq has pulled the bitcoin price lower even when on-chain flows looked steady. Miners that leased power and floor space to AI tenants are caught in both trades.

Fund flows followed the risk-off tape. U.S. spot bitcoin ETFs posted $430.2 million in net outflows on Thursday, led by about $200 million from Fidelity’s FBTC and roughly $105 million from BlackRock’s IBIT, bringing week-to-date redemptions through Thursday to about $870 million.

Leverage kept the bitcoin price soft into the overnight. More than $1 billion in crypto positions were liquidated over 24 hours, mostly long bets built during Wednesday’s short-squeeze bounce that lifted bitcoin near $80,000.

Professional and business services led October hiring with 70,000 jobs, health care added 42,000, and transportation and warehousing gained 25,000. Manufacturing rose 19,000. The breadth undercuts the recent pattern in which health care carried most of the gains.

Policy risk has not eased either. House Democrats are already drafting their own market structure bill ahead of taking the committees in January, a backdrop that has hung over crypto equities since the midterm results first hit the bitcoin price.

Traders are watching whether the bitcoin price can defend the mid-$75,000 zone that held on election night and several times since August. A clean break would leave September’s low back in sight after a week that erased most of the post-election squeeze, now with a hotter labor market and firmer hike odds stacked on top.

At the time of writing, the bitcoin price is near $75,500.

#5214. Stablecoin contraction forces Bitcoin drop despite Fed reserve gains

The dated comparison pairs a reserve rebound with shrinking dollar-token circulation, leaving the link to Bitcoin buying unestablished.

Bank reserve balances rose $67.5 billion between Oct. 28 and Nov. 4, according to the Federal Reserve’s Nov. 5 release, but the more abundant dollar liquidity is not automatically bullish for Bitcoin.

Over the same period, data provider DefiLlama tracked dollar-pegged stablecoin circulation falling by more than 5.7 billion units, or 1.91%.

The split leaves an incomplete picture for a Bitcoin recovery. Higher bank reserve balances would still need to translate into financing and purchases, so the measured token contraction adds a crypto-side observation to that test.

A Nov. 5 Treasury-cash analysis considered a possible December funding window using projected cash paths and the older Oct. 28 reserve baseline. The new release updates that starting point with a rebound in realized reserves.

Coinbase's completed Nov. 5 daily BTC-USD candle closed at $75,969.06, down 5.54% from $80,428.31 on Nov. 4. That close-to-close fall followed the dated reserve observation, putting the price move outside the bank-balance comparison window.

The Fed’s H.4.1 release puts Nov. 4 reserve balances at $2.96 trillion. Over the preceding week, the Treasury General Account, the government’s account at the Fed, fell $79.4 billion to $936.2 billion.

Treasury payments move balances into depository institutions’ accounts, increasing reserves all else equal, while receipts flowing into Treasury’s account work in the opposite direction.

The final reserve change also incorporates movements elsewhere on the Fed’s balance sheet. Total assets rose $2.5 billion, while other balance-sheet changes offset part of the effect of the Treasury-account drawdown.

Tether’s USDT series fell 3.65 billion units, accounting for 63.37% of that measured contraction, while USD Coin (USDC) circulation declined 383 million units, contributing 6.64%. Other tracked dollar-pegged tokens together contracted a net 1.73 billion units.

The issuer breakdown locates the largest measured change in USDT. Whether, and to what extent, issuer redemptions, bridge-accounting adjustments, reclassification, or coverage revisions contributed remains unquantified.

#5213. S&P 500 Plunges, Wall Street Fears Circuit Breaker, Just One Day After Trump Celebrated Record Highs

S&P 500 Plunges, Wall Street Fears Circuit Breaker, Just One Day After Trump Celebrated Record Highs

President Donald Trump's celebration of Wall Street's highs aged rather poorly. Less than 24 hours after boasting about Americans' retirement accounts going "through the roof," the S&P 500 plunged as much as 3.8% Thursday, bringing emergency trading halts into the conversation.

The selloff prompted traders to monitor the market's circuit-breaker thresholds, which can temporarily shut down trading across U.S. stock exchanges when losses become too severe. The S&P 500 would need to fall another 3.2 percentage points from its decline to trigger the first emergency halt.

The timing is particularly awkward for Trump, who has repeatedly pointed to stock market performance as evidence of his administration's economic success.

"Stock Market just hit another Record High—91 Days in just a short period of time—Less than two years. Your 401(k)s are through the roof," Trump wrote Wednesday on his Truth Social account. He closed the message with "Record Setting All! President DONALD J. TRUMP."

Wall Street had rallied Wednesday following the Democratic Party's sweeping midterm election victories. The S&P 500 gained 1.6%, its strongest daily performance in three months, while the Dow Jones Industrial Average jumped more than 1,500 points to 53.361 points.

Thursday's losses erase that rally in a single session, with technology stocks taking the biggest beating.

The U.S. stock market has three emergency thresholds designed to slow panic selling. These mechanisms, known as circuit breakers, automatically suspend trading when the S&P 500 falls by a specified percentage from its previous closing price.

The first threshold is a 7% decline, corresponding to an S&P 500 level of 7,150.41 points for Thursday's session. Reaching that level before 3:25 p.m. ET would trigger a 15-minute halt across US equity markets.

The circuit breaker level is located in the following chart’s blue line

A 13% decline, or 6,689.09 points, would trigger another 15-minute suspension. The most severe threshold comes at 20%, or 6,150.89 points, which would shut down trading for the remainder of the day.

Under the New York Stock Exchange's rules, the first two circuit breakers stop applying at 3:25 p.m. ET, while the 20% threshold remains active throughout the session.

The rules apply to the entire U.S. equity market, not just stocks included in the S&P 500. A market-wide halt would also suspend trading in companies listed on the Nasdaq and other US exchanges.

The technology sector was particularly hard hit as investors continued digesting the consequences of Tuesday's election results. At around 11 a.m. ET, the Nasdaq Composite had fallen nearly 5%, while the S&P 500 was down 3.2%.

Nvidia lost more than 6%, while the broader semiconductor index plunged nearly 11%. Companies involved in building AI infrastructure were also under pressure as investors worried that Democratic victories could bring stricter oversight of data-center construction.

"We suspect there is going to be some scrutiny on this aggressive AI buildout," Ipek Ozkardeskaya, senior analyst at Swissquote Bank, told Reuters.

The selloff also spilled into cryptocurrencies. Bitcoin fell below $76,000, wiping out its post-election recovery as approximately $428 million in leveraged crypto long positions were liquidated within an hour, as Decrypt reported earlier today.

Stocks also faced pressure from persistent inflation concerns and uncertainty over the Federal Reserve's next interest-rate decision. Investors are awaiting Friday's October employment report, which could provide further clues about the direction of monetary policy.

Despite Thursday's sharp losses, the S&P 500 had not reached its first market-wide circuit breaker at the time of this report.

#5209. MEXC sees $126 million outflows as US targets exchange over $16.7 billion in suspected illicit transactions

MEXC says trading remains normal and customer assets are secure, but the Treasury's latest action could complicate its banking relationships.

Crypto exchange MEXC saw $126 million in net outflows as US regulators moved to sever its access to the American financial system.

The withdrawals coincided with a Nov. 6 enforcement action by the Treasury's Financial Crimes Enforcement Network (FinCEN), which accused the exchange of facilitating $16.7 billion in transactions linked to sanctions evasion, terrorist financing and cybercrime.

Data from DeFiLlama showed $126.03 million in net outflows over a 24-hour period spanning the announcement, while the exchange retained approximately $4.353 billion in tracked assets.

This represent net movements through monitored wallets and do not establish that all withdrawals occurred after the announcement or indicate whether the exchange has sufficient assets to meet its customer liabilities.

MEXC has moved to contain concerns about its financial position, insisting that customer funds remain secure and its trading, deposit and withdrawal services are operating normally.

In an official statement, the exchange said it was reviewing the Treasury order with its legal and compliance teams and had initiated an independent examination of its anti-money laundering and sanctions controls.

“We take the concerns raised seriously and intend to engage constructively with FinCEN, as well as other relevant regulators and competent authorities worldwide.”

CEO Vugar Usi separately sought to clarify the implications of the order after concerns spread among users.

“A number of misconceptions are floating around,” Usi said, emphasizing that the restrictions apply to transactions involving US financial institutions rather than the exchange's entire global operation.

“The order itself does not freeze or seize any assets held on MEXC. Users can trade normally.”

Usi expressed confidence that the matter would be resolved and said the exchange would continue updating customers as discussions with regulators progress.

The response followed a sweeping investigation that identified significant deficiencies in MEXC's compliance controls despite its stated policies against servicing customers in sanctioned jurisdictions.

In its 24-page order, FinCEN said MEXC facilitated approximately $16.7 billion in likely illicit activity between May 2021 and May 2026, including transactions involving Russian sanctions evasion networks and Iranian-linked entities.

Nearly $1.5 billion involved direct or indirect exposure to Russian sanctions evasion, including $232 million in direct dealings with sanctioned entities.

FinCEN specifically identified approximately $129 million in transactions involving Garantex and another $4.1 million involving Grinex after the Russian exchanges were sanctioned by Western authorities.

The agency said MEXC continued transacting with both exchanges as recently as June 2026.

Investigators also identified more than $1.87 billion in transactions with Iranian exposure, including approximately $1 billion in direct transfers involving more than 40 Iranian crypto exchanges.

Another $22 million involved Iran's central bank and the Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps, including its Qods Force.

Separately, the agency identified at least $1.99 billion involving illicit cyber actors, including more than $37.9 million in suspected ransomware payments.

Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent framed the enforcement action as part of Washington's broader effort to restrict financial channels used by sanctioned actors. He said:

“Either you support the Iranian regime, or you have access to the U.S. financial system.”

The order prohibits covered US financial institutions from sending or receiving funds involving MEXC, including cryptocurrency transfers, beginning 30 days after its publication in the Federal Register.

It has no scheduled expiration date, although FinCEN retains the authority to grant exemptions.

The restriction does not shut down MEXC internationally or automatically freeze customer assets, but it could complicate the exchange's relationships with banks, financial intermediaries and liquidity providers that operate within the US regulatory system.

The US enforcement action comes as MEXC retreats from several international markets amid mounting regulatory scrutiny, even as its trading business continues to expand.

As CryptoSlate reported on Oct. 30, the exchange has announced withdrawals from France, the Netherlands, Russia and Belarus as part of its efforts to address compliance concerns.

The latest US intervention adds to those setbacks, potentially complicating MEXC's ambitions to expand beyond cryptocurrency trading into traditional financial markets.

The regulatory pressure comes despite the exchange's growing market presence.

A TokenInsight report showed that MEXC processed $1.069 trillion in trading volume during the third quarter of 2026, ranking fourth among exchanges covered by the study.

Its overall market share reached 8.91%, up 0.38 percentage points from the previous quarter.

MEXC has also gained ground in derivatives linked to traditional financial assets, processing $86 billion in commodity perpetual futures during the quarter. That gave it a 17.43% market share, second only to Binance.

The figures highlight the scale of the trading business now exposed to heightened regulatory uncertainty as MEXC attempts to broaden its presence in global financial markets.

Although FinCEN's order does not prohibit MEXC from operating internationally, it could disrupt relationships with banks, liquidity providers and other intermediaries subject to US jurisdiction.

The extent of that disruption remains unclear because MEXC has not disclosed how much of its trading activity or liquidity depends on institutions covered by the prohibition.

The immediate test will come when the restrictions take effect, particularly whether the exchange can maintain its existing financial relationships while addressing the compliance deficiencies identified by US authorities.

#5207. Binance Hackers Swap About $50 Million of Stolen XRP Mostly Into Bitcoin, Elliptic Says

Hackers tied to the record Binance theft have begun converting a large slice of the stolen XRP into bitcoin, blockchain analytics firm Elliptic said in an update dated through Friday, with about 42 million XRP — roughly $50 million — already swapped, mostly through THORChain.

Converted bitcoin from other stolen assets nearly doubled overnight to about 1,650 BTC, worth some $124 million, Elliptic wrote. Most of that jump came from the XRP route. About 192 million XRP, roughly $226 million, remained unswapped in addresses linked to the Binance theft.

The XRP was part of the Oct. 29 drain that Binance later put at $6.34 billion. Between 20:05 and 20:09 UTC that day, five hot wallets sent 233.46 million XRP to an attacker address. The FBI has attributed the campaign to North Korea’s TraderTraitor group. Binance has reopened withdrawals in stages and said it has frozen or intercepted about $2.85 billion while covering customer losses from its own funds.

The stolen XRP sat quiet at first. Early on Nov. 2, between 02:12 and 04:00 UTC, all about 233 million XRP moved into six new addresses, where it stayed until Nov. 5, Elliptic said. From the afternoon of Nov. 5, two of those six addresses began sending XRP on to dozens of new accounts that swapped it, mostly through THORChain into bitcoin.

A small amount of ether also went through THORChain into bitcoin that evening. The main bitcoin and ether holdings from the theft — the largest pools — have still not moved, Elliptic said. That pattern matches what the firm saw after the Bybit hack: turn freezable and mid-liquidity tokens into bitcoin first, then attempt cash-out later through OTC brokers.

The broader laundering trail is already vast. Since the Binance theft, Elliptic has counted more than 20,000 wallets on 16 blockchains, at least 36 services and at least 65,000 transactions — roughly one new wallet every 30 seconds and one transaction every 10 seconds. About $3.11 billion remains in addresses linked to the Binance theft, mostly as ether and bitcoin. A negligible amount has likely been cashed out so far.

Issuers can freeze many stablecoins. They cannot freeze XRP the same way, and they cannot freeze bitcoin. After Tether and Circle locked down large stablecoin tranches on Oct. 29, the attacker prioritized assets that sit outside that kill switch. Swapping XRP into bitcoin continues that logic: bitcoin is harder to claw back once it is fragmented across fresh addresses.

All of the stolen SOL, about $87 million at Elliptic’s prices, has already been swapped, bridged or mixed, with much of it ending as bitcoin. Most early Tron laundering — about $220 million in TRX — has also run, a flow Bitcoin Magazine noted when covering the quiet shutdown of the T3 Financial Crime Unit before the hack. Only small parts of the main ether and NEAR piles have moved.

U.S. bank supervisors have already checked in with lenders after the Binance breach. For bitcoin markets, the nearer question is how much more of the remaining ~192 million XRP — and eventually the parked ether — gets turned into BTC before anyone tries to cash out.

The test now is whether THORChain operators and other bridges keep blocking the published attacker addresses fast enough to slow the XRP-to-bitcoin pipe, or whether the rest of that pile follows the same overnight path the first $50 million already took.

#5204. Wall Street can stop a tokenized share, but can it stop a stock perp?

Wall Street is fighting over crypto-style stock trading as major companies reject tokenized shares and traditional exchanges challenge new derivatives markets.

Costco recently made news when it strongly objected to a blockchain version of its shares. And while the supermarket giant did raise a set of pretty valid concerns regarding the potential risks of tokenization, the problem was that traders can actually already buy a blockchain-based contract (i.e., token) that follows its stock price.

The company is among 67 issuers in OKXICE's Nov. 5 objection notice, blocking third-party tokenized versions of their stock from the proposed venue backed by the New York Stock Exchange's owner.

Meanwhile, Kalshi lists a Costco perpetual, which offers price exposure without giving its buyer any shares.

The two products show how differently finance treats owning a company and trading its price: an issuer can stop its stock tokens from trading through one blockchain arrangement, while contracts tied to that stock follow a separate legal route.

That route is now being challenged in court, with established Wall Street firms competing to offer these contracts.

Shares give shareholders an interest in a business, along with the voting and distribution rights attached to the particular class of stock they own. Putting that interest on a blockchain can change how it's transferred while preserving what its owner is entitled to receive.

OKXICE's proposed model uses a tokenization provider acting through a registered broker-dealer to hold one underlying share for each token. Each token represents an entitlement to that share, and customers would trade them against dollar-linked stablecoins in automated asset pools.

Those pools can produce different prices from conventional exchanges. The ownership claim and the price you can get for it are separate concerns.

The Securities and Exchange Commission's September exemption allows qualifying venues to operate outside ordinary exchange registration, subject to conditions.

Tokens must preserve equivalent shareholder rights, including votes, dividends, and a claim on residual assets if the company is liquidated. Any token that just copies a share price doesn't qualify.

But the underlying company also gets a say. Before offering stock tokenized by an unaffiliated third party, an exchange has to notify the issuer, and each company has 30 days from receiving its notice to object.

The SEC's staff guidance confirms that a timely objection must be disclosed and prevents the venue from offering that token under the exemption.

Costco and dozens of others have already exercised that right, with their objections applying to OKXICE, and the companies haven't collectively explained their motives. The list therefore tells us little about their views on other forms of tokenization or contracts referencing their share prices.

A Costco perp works differently. You go long to gain when the stock rises, or short to gain when it falls. The contract has no scheduled expiry, and Kalshi's stock-perps documentation says holders receive neither shares nor voting rights.

Reported trailing 24-hour notional volume from one Kalshi data snapshot retrieved Nov. 6, 2026, at 13:49 UTC, rounded to the nearest $1,000. Covers only the four stocks named in Kalshi's Nov. 3 launch announcement, not its full stock-perp market. The exact rolling-window cutoff is not separately timestamped. Turnover is not investor count, new capital, or a measure of bullish demand.

Source: Kalshi market data, retrieved Nov. 6, 2026, 13:49 UTC · Kalshi's Nov. 3 stock-perpetual launch announcement

That also means you can take substantial exposure with relatively little money. Costco's market page showed maximum leverage of 4.9 times on Nov. 6, meaning $1,000 of collateral supports $4,900 of price exposure.

A 10% move would cost $490 before fees and other adjustments, nearly half the original collateral. If collateral falls below the required minimum, the platform can close the position.

Even though the contract has no expiry, you may lose the ability to wait for a recovery.

Thanks for signing up to The Catalyst. Look out for your first daily email. If you already receive it, you’re all set.

Hypothetical arithmetic, not observed returns or a forecast. Start with $1,000: an unlevered stock purchase has $1,000 exposure; a 4.9x long perp has $4,900. Assumes identical percentage price moves and unchanged position size. Loss = starting exposure x price decline. Excludes fees, funding, dividends, lot-size rounding and other adjustments. Liquidation could occur before an illustrated decline, so the bars are not a path an actual position can necessarily survive. The 4.9x input is Costco's displayed maximum on Nov. 6, 2026.

Source: Kalshi Costco market: displayed maximum leverage on Nov. 6, 2026 · Kalshi stock-perp mechanics and liquidation risks; illustrative calculations by CryptoSlate

Perps also need a way to keep their price near the underlying asset. Conventional futures have an eventual settlement date, while perpetuals use recurring payments between traders, called funding, to discourage a persistent gap.

When the contract trades too high relative to its reference price, longs have to pay shorts, and when it trades too low, payments run the other way.

These funding transfers can reward taking the less popular side, but they also mean that correctly predicting a stock's direction doesn't fully determine your return.

Kalshi says cash dividends generate contractual cash adjustments for long and short positions, although those payments don't make contract holders shareholders, nor does perpetual mean permanently open.

Kalshi's Costco page lists trading from Sunday evening to Friday afternoon, Eastern time. Its documentation warns that customers cannot open or close positions during closures.

On Nov. 3, CFTC Chairman Michael Selig said equity perpetuals could begin trading on eligible exchanges registered with the derivatives regulator and notice-registered with the SEC. He described the products as deemed approved under Regulation 40.3, which permits approval when the review period expires without a notice of non-approval.

The agency treats them as security futures, placing them under a joint regulatory framework. Their lack of an expiry date is at the heart of the challenge to that classification.

The Capital Markets Alliance Association, whose court filings include declarations from Cboe and Citadel Securities, wants to stop the approvals. It argues that a contract without future delivery cannot qualify as a security future.

In an October SEC submission, Cboe argued that the contracts fall under security-based swaps, agreements that transfer financial exposure linked to securities under different rules. It also warned that stock-perp trades would escape the securities markets' consolidated surveillance system even when traders hedge them with ordinary shares.

Cboe has a commercial stake in the outcome, but its argument raises a substantive issue about which safeguards should follow a trade that tracks a stock without delivering it.

Judge Valerie Caproni's Nov. 3 order found the challengers likely to succeed in arguing that the CFTC exceeded its authority. She nevertheless denied an emergency restraining order because the alliance hadn't demonstrated the imminent, irreparable harm required for that relief.

The approvals remained undisturbed at that stage, despite the judge's preliminary assessment of the CFTC's legal position. The order set Nov. 6 for the government's opposition to a preliminary injunction and Nov. 10 for the alliance's reply.

Meanwhile, the NYSE's parent is pursuing both markets. ICE announced an investment and agreement with Trade[XYZ] on Oct. 29 to bring its Hyperliquid-based perpetual markets into the US.

The planned regulated venue still requires approvals, including for leveraged trading. ICE expects distribution to include its OKX joint venture, subject to that venture's own approvals.

The same exchange group backing tokenized shares sees an opportunity in selling exposure to their prices. The dispute is partly over which institutions can offer crypto's trading model, and which rules they must follow.

The difference for investors is what each product promises. Qualifying share tokens must preserve shareholder rights while introducing the costs and risks of their trading platform. Perps offer leveraged exposure governed by collateral requirements, funding payments, and contractual adjustments.

Costco's objection shows how an issuer can stop the first product on a particular venue, while the court challenge concerns regulators' authority to approve the second. The ticker may be familiar, but the rights and risks come from the contract you buy.
