#5098. Ex-FBI agent pleads guilty to stealing $2.47 million in crypto from wallets tied to 35 investigations

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

Court filings show Patrick Yaroch drained at least 79 wallets, including some belonging to crime victims, and planned to use the money to buy a vineyard in Portugal.

Former FBI agent Patrick Steven Yaroch admitted on Thursday to stealing about $2.47 million in cryptocurrency from wallets tied to FBI investigations, according to the U.S. Attorney's Office for the Eastern District of Virginia.

Yaroch, 37, of Ashburn, Virginia, pleaded guilty to one count each of wire fraud and money laundering related to the thefts. Each count carries up to 20 years in prison, and sentencing is set for March 4, 2027.

Beginning no later than December 2024, Yaroch searched FBI databases, including a classified one, for crypto credentials, according to the statement of facts he signed. He used the information he found to make about 100 transfers out of at least 79 wallets connected to 35 FBI investigations.

Most of the wallets belonged to people under FBI investigation, and some were controlled by two adversarial nation states the filing does not name. NBC News reported in August, citing two sources, that the adversarial nation in the original complaint was Russia. Yaroch also made at least 10 transfers from wallets belonging to crime victims in other FBI cases, and at least one from a wallet the FBI had funded for an operation.

The filing calls Yaroch "indiscriminate," with individual thefts ranging from 81 cents to more than $580,000. After each theft, he kept checking FBI systems to make sure no one had noticed.

Yaroch joined the FBI's Boston Division in 2017, working on a squad focused on one of the two nation states. From February 2025 until July 31, 2026, he was a supervisory special agent in the Counterintelligence and Espionage Division at FBI headquarters.

Yaroch had racked up significant debts before the scheme began: he had taken out more than $100,000 in loans in 2024 and owed more than $45,000 on credit cards, according to the filing.

Between Nov. 25, 2025, and Jan. 13, 2026, Yaroch moved about $93,082 to a TD Bank account and used it to pay down debts, including about $30,176 on credit cards and $20,000 toward a Lexus, per the filing. Yaroch initially said he stole the crypto out of frustration, but prosecutors wrote in an August court filing that he "should not be viewed as a selfless Robin Hoodesque figure."

On June 4, Yaroch asked ChatGPT what it would do with about $1 million if it wanted to leave the U.S. and live in a European Union country. The chatbot's answer cited his wife and child, "the desire for a 2-5 hectare estate, interest in age-worthy red wine," and named Portugal "my top choice for your specific situation," according to the filing.

On June 15, Yaroch gave two Portuguese lawyers power of attorney to register him with the country's tax authority. He also arranged a September trip to Portugal tied to his planned vineyard purchase. Prosecutors also said in the August filing that Yaroch had failed to report a 10-day trip in May, when he flew to Germany and came home from Portugal.

The prosecutors asked to keep him jailed before trial, citing about $160,000 he still held on Kraken and possible Espionage Act charges that were never filed. U.S. District Judge Anthony Trenga instead released him on a $50,000 bond with location monitoring, under conditions that bar him from using video game consoles.

Yaroch initially agreed to give agents a piece of paper that appeared to contain his seed phrase, according to the August filing. However, he later asked for it back after agents told him he had the right to stay silent and that what he said could be used against him in a criminal case.

When agents found the wallet he had pointed them to, $1.02 million had already moved out. Yaroch then admitted the funds were on the lending platform Suilend and helped them reinstall a wallet app he had deleted from his phone.

Under the plea agreement, Yaroch must pay at least $2.4 million in restitution and faces a forfeiture judgment of at least $2.4 million. He also agreed to forfeit 17,990.67 USDC and about 1.38 million SUI seized on July 31, worth roughly $1.2 million at current prices, plus the contents of his TD Bank, Charles Schwab, Kraken and BloFin accounts.

Yaroch acknowledged that the full proceeds of the scheme "are not available." He also agreed to be interviewed by Justice Department and FBI officials about his crimes.

If there's eventually a Netflix documentary about the case, Yaroch won't be profiting: any money he makes from books, films, articles or interviews about his FBI work or his case will go to the U.S. government. He must also submit any such work to the FBI for review before publication.

The Block reported in August that Yaroch had been fired on July 31 and charged with transporting and receiving stolen money. At the time, the Justice Department's Office of the Inspector General said he had moved about $1 million in roughly 10 transfers and had reported himself to two Justice Department employees on July 29.

Earlier this year, the FBI arrested John Daghita in Saint Martin over the alleged theft of more than $46 million in crypto from the U.S. Marshals Service. Onchain investigator ZachXBT alleged that Daghita is the son of an executive at a firm contracted to help the agency manage seized crypto.

A federal grand jury in Alexandria indicted Daghita on 15 counts in March, and a French appeals court in Guadeloupe approved his extradition to the U.S. in May, according to local broadcaster RCI. His arraignment is set for Nov. 12 in Alexandria, according to his court docket.

#5096. US Sanctions 45 More Targets Tied to Alleged Crypto Scam Empire Prince Group

US Sanctions 45 More Targets Tied to Alleged Crypto Scam Empire Prince Group

The U.S. Treasury Department has sanctioned four individuals and 41 entities that it says support Prince Group, the Cambodian conglomerate accused of running scam compounds that use trafficked workers to defraud victims through crypto investment and romance scams.

The new designations, announced on Thursday by the Office of Foreign Assets Control (OFAC), target the offshore companies, nominee owners and administrators that Treasury says have helped Prince Group executives conceal assets and evade earlier sanctions imposed in October 2025 and June 2026.

"Cyber scam syndicates continue to steal billions of dollars from American victims while relying on sophisticated international business networks to launder their illicit proceeds," Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent said.

Among those sanctioned is Qiu Yong, who Treasury said controls a network of British Virgin Islands and Hong Kong companies on behalf of Prince Group, including money lenders and wine businesses, and who has worked for a company controlled by Prince Group chairman Chen Zhi. His personal assistant, Zhao Mengyao, was also designated.

OFAC also targeted two people it said are holding property for, or running companies with, Prince Group executive Chen Bo, who was sanctioned in June. They include his wife, He Yujing, to whom Chen Bo transferred ownership of his Tokyo mansion after the October 2025 sanctions, according to Treasury, and associate Visal Pang, who controls 11 Cambodian companies spanning construction, logistics, a hotel and a "super app." Eleven companies still controlled by Chen Bo were also sanctioned, including Prince International Airlines and CB General Insurance.

Further entities in Singapore, Mauritius and Malaysia were sanctioned for links to Prince Group associate Dai An, along with a Singapore media company owned by alleged Prince Group finance chief Thet Li.

Separately, OFAC designated Chui Kok Wa, a Malaysian national based in Madagascar, who Treasury said led a network of scammers that used crypto and other technologies to defraud Americans of more than $400,000.

The U.S. is seeking the forfeiture of about $14 billion in Bitcoin tied to Prince Group, while UK authorities have frozen the London penthouse of Thet Li, Decrypt reported last month. Prince Group has denied the allegations.

Chen Zhi himself was arrested in Cambodia in January along with two other Chinese nationals and extradited to China, where state media described him as the head of a major cross-border gambling and fraud syndicate, the BBC reported. Cambodia, which had revoked his citizenship the month before, also placed the group's Prince Bank into liquidation.

Thursday's action was coordinated with the FBI, the Justice Department's Scam Center Strike Force, which has reported $580 million in crypto seizures and freezes, and the U.S. Attorney's Office for the District of Alaska, with help from authorities in the British Virgin Islands and the UK, according to Treasury.

Interpol declared scam compound networks a global threat last year, citing their use of human trafficking, online fraud and coerced labor. In September, the Treasury's Financial Crimes Enforcement Network linked about $12.7 billion in reported financial activity to suspected crypto investment scams, largely run by criminal organizations operating scam compounds in Southeast Asia.

The UN Office on Drugs and Crime estimated in July that scam operations across East and Southeast Asia, Australia and New Zealand cost victims between $88.3 billion and $114.1 billion in 2025, much of it through crypto investment fraud run from compounds holding people from at least 80 countries.

#5095. Binance’s $6.3B hack leaves questions over hot wallet safeguards

Experts say blockchain records show repeated wallet drains but cannot establish Binance’s internal signing controls or transaction limits.

Binance’s $6.3 billion security incident has left unanswered questions about how the exchange managed its hot wallets and limited the amount attackers could access.

Binance co-CEO Richard Teng said on Oct. 30 that the attack affected the exchange’s own hot wallet. Most customer assets were held in offline cold storage and moved to hot wallets only when users requested withdrawals.

Wallet balances offer only a partial picture of an exchange’s exposure to an attack. Public blockchain records can show funds moving but cannot establish the internal controls governing those transfers, CertiK analyst Jonathan Riss told Cointelegraph.

In its initial report Tuesday, Binance said attackers accessed an internal wallet-signing service to move funds outside its normal withdrawal process. Binance said some technical details would remain confidential to prevent further attacks.

The exchange did not respond to Cointelegraph’s questions about its wallet balances and safeguards.

Rivals including Coinbase, Gate and Upbit have disclosed their cold-storage ratios following the attack.

Riss said the $6.3 billion figure represented the cumulative value of assets affected across 10 blockchains. On Ethereum, attackers repeatedly drained a hot wallet as new deposits arrived.

When attackers can obtain valid signatures, their potential access depends on what the signing system allows them to transfer over time, he said. A wallet’s balance at a single moment does not capture that exposure.

Related: EIP-7702 didn’t cause Binance hack, but may have amplified losses

Transaction limits enforced by the application requesting a signature can be bypassed if that application is compromised, Riss said. Limits enforced independently by the signing system may still restrict transfers.

“A comparison with other exchanges isn’t really possible from the outside,” Riss added. “Public information on internal signing architecture is limited everywhere, and reserve distribution and proof-of-reserves attestations show what sits where, not how a withdrawal is authorized.”

BitGo chief product officer Eugene Hahr also declined to speculate about Binance’s internal safeguards.

“The breakdown of custody arrangements is a question Binance is best placed to answer,” he said.

Idan Ofrat, co-founder and chief product officer at Fireblocks, said exchanges typically keep enough funds in hot wallets to cover a few days of expected withdrawals, often representing a low single-digit percentage of assets under management.

Exchanges generally hold the bulk of customer assets in cold storage, while some use intermediate wallets that require manual approval for transfers to hot wallets, he said.

Related: Binance traces $6.3B hack to fake job interviews, compromised AI tool

Alex Tobol, chief technology officer at Telegram-based crypto wallet Walt, said holding billions of dollars in hot wallets is not inherently unusual for an exchange of Binance’s size.

Hot wallets generally process withdrawals automatically, Tobol said, but replenishing them from cold storage should require a separate treasury decision and manual approval.

Hahr said controls applied before signing, including transaction limits, approved withdrawal addresses and additional authorization requirements, can help contain losses when a system is compromised.

Magazine: Binance hackers raced to move billions before it could be frozen

#5094. 7 Pendant Lights to Perfect Your Overhead Lighting Scheme (2026)

Sure, recessed lighting serves a functional purpose, but pendant lights are where things get interesting. A quick sweep through any home tour, and you’ll recognize the strategically placed Louis Poulsen above the kitchen island, an Isamu Noguchi in the entryway, and a Herman Miller saucer pendant hovering over the living room that will steal the show. Below are seven pendant light fixtures that set the mood instead of harshening the vibe. We rounded up the best pendant lights for various design personalities, including a vintage-inspired showpiece and a few variations of low-key ambient lighting. Among them are most of the different types of pendant lights you’re likely to come across, with picks for modern minimalists, Belle Époque enthusiasts, and everyone in between.

First developed in 1958, the PH 5 is a classic for a reason. Renowned Danish lighting manufacturer Louis Poulsen designed the iconic pendant with a three-shade system to equally distribute light. While the pendant works for that functional purpose, it also doubles as an unconventional art piece. Choose from a number of monochromatic and multitone colorways, including this steel blue.

Look familiar? That’s because this large pendant, inspired by Noguchi’s Akaris, hangs in many an AD home tour, including above the dining room table in this Japandi-inspired Bay Area home and in the bedroom of this South African–inspired craft home, for example. There are a few reasons why this style of paper lighting is so popular: It goes with everything; it’s affordable; and it adds a sense of levity to the room with its ethereal shape and its soft, warm glow. As lantern pendants go, this option from Design Within Reach is about as low-commitment as they come. It works anywhere you want ambient lighting rather than a spotlight.

Designer pendant lights can cost upwards of $500, but you can pay a fraction of that with finds from Article, a direct-to-consumer furniture store that sells high-quality wares at more palatable prices. That’s certainly the case with the Aino pendant, with matte colorways and a simple angular silhouette that illuminates whatever you’re chef-ing up in your Scandi-style kitchen. The metal cone shade and braided fabric cord make it an easy pick for task lighting over a counter, and if you’re working with a tighter kitchen space, there’s also a mini pendant version for $99 that ships with an LED bulb.

This three-tiered ceiling light from West Elm adds visual texture to blank space, making it a welcome addition to transitional areas in need of personality. A piece like this can also help to add structure to an open plan where the kitchen and dining rooms collide. This piece comes in 26-inch and 32-inch sizes (the larger is a proper large pendant light that can hold its own over a dining table), as well as in hardwired and plug-in options, making it one of the few statement hanging lights you can install without calling an electrician.

Any cold kitchen space dominated by stone and steel fixtures could use a touch of handmade whimsy. Hanging this handwoven rattan pendant over a kitchen island promises to warm up the whole room. Usually, two or three pendant lights hang over a kitchen island; this single shade provides a cozy, welcoming alternative, softening the light rather than spotlighting the countertop, so your mise en place won’t feel so interrogated.

This midcentury Herman Miller pendant, aptly named a saucer for its elliptical shape, is prized by commerce writer Julia Harrison for its retro, Mad Men–esque appeal. Despite the resemblance to our Hay pick above, Miller wasn’t riffing on Noguchi with this pendant: He wanted a Swedish silk-covered pendant he couldn’t justify buying, so he made his own using a sprayed plastic skin originally developed for mothballing Navy ships (whereas the orbital Hay version uses rice paper). The vertical ribbing gives it a shape that seems to be almost expanding as it hangs. Its white shade and chrome hardware make it easily adaptable to many (if not most) design styles.

This lighting fixture brings to mind 10-foot ceilings, Flemish tapestries, and an echoey marble entryway. If you’re lucky enough to have all of the above, this is exactly the sort of pendant your space needs. If not, luckily, the fixture carries that whole vibe on its own, with its cream glass bowl, brass detailing, and a sculpted metal tassel that adds a bit of movement. A vintage-inspired piece like this transforms a more humble hallway into something dignified and scenic.

#5093. Bitcoin ETFs Bleed $430 Million as Tech Rout Tests Wall Street Buyers

Bitcoin ETFs Bleed $430 Million as Tech Rout Tests Wall Street Buyers

U.S. spot Bitcoin exchange-traded funds lost $430.2 million to net withdrawals Thursday as a technology-stock selloff spread to crypto, with Fidelity and BlackRock leading the exits.

Fidelity's Wise Origin Bitcoin Fund (FBTC) posted $199.9 million in net outflows and BlackRock's iShares Bitcoin Trust (IBIT) shed $105 million, according to Farside Investors. Together they accounted for about 71% of the day's withdrawals.

Grayscale's GBTC lost another $49.9 million, while Franklin Templeton's EZBC shed $32.9 million. No fund in Farside's table recorded positive net flows Thursday, although several reported no net movement.

The latest withdrawals brought this week's net outflow to $869.8 million, following a $643.2 million outflow last Friday. Wednesday's modest $74.6 million inflow offered a brief reprieve before Thursday's reversal.

Fidelity's swing was particularly sharp: FBTC attracted $236.7 million Wednesday, then surrendered nearly $200 million the next session. BlackRock, meanwhile, lost $201.2 million even on Wednesday's positive day, extending a five-session withdrawal streak since October 30 to roughly $1.04 billion.

ETF flows have often been treated as a gauge of demand from investors accessing Bitcoin through regular brokerage accounts. The latest figures show that access works both ways: the same familiar wrapper that makes buying easy also makes exiting straightforward.

Because ETFs trade on stock exchanges, investors can adjust their Bitcoin exposure without transferring coins to a crypto wallet. That convenience can also make the funds an easy place to cut risk during an equity selloff. Daily net flows capture shares created or redeemed at the fund level, not every trade between investors.

Thursday's redemptions arrived as the Nasdaq Composite plunged 5.5% and the S&P 500 fell about 4.4%. Chipmakers and data-center stocks led the decline as investors questioned the pace of artificial-intelligence spending and potential restrictions after the U.S. midterm elections.

Bitcoin also surrendered its post-election rebound, falling back toward $76,000 after trading above $80,000 Wednesday. Short liquidations had helped power that rally, making the subsequent reversal more painful for leveraged traders.

The ETF figures add another piece to that picture. Net flows measure creations minus redemptions of ETF shares; they cannot tell whether holders were raising cash to cover losses elsewhere, trimming Bitcoin exposure, or rebalancing portfolios.

#5092. Paraguay Protests Over Bank Lockout Intensify as Impeachment Vote Nears

Paraguay Protests Over Bank Lockout Intensify as Impeachment Vote Nears

Thousands of Paraguayans marched through Asunción this week demanding their money back after Ueno Bank, a digital lender, locked about 3 million customers out of their accounts, according.

That is about 60% of Paraguay’s adults. The bank also held $800 million to $850 million in public money, including state pension and health funds, according to the AP, so the fallout reaches the pension system.

Anger boiled over Thursday night outside Ueno’s headquarters in Asunción, where savers burned debit and credit cards. Paraguayo “Payo” Cubas, a former presidential candidate, tried to join and was booed, shoved, and forced out.

Protesters shouted “divisor” and “oportunista,” meaning divider and opportunist, accusing him of trying to profit politically from public rage, according to the same report.

Diario Hoy reported that protesters also blamed him for the Senate’s current makeup, saying Cubas put Zenaida Delgado and Javier Vera on his party’s legislative ticket, the slate of candidates voters choose from, and that both joined the government’s side soon after taking office.

#Nacionales | 😱 Echan a "Payo" Cubas de la manifestación

🔹 Paraguayo “Payo” Cubas fue rechazado por los manifestantes cuando intentó acercarse a la protesta. Los presentes lo increparon y le gritaron “divisor” y “oportunista”.

➡️ Además, cuestionaron su responsabilidad en el… pic.twitter.com/odHzVvGPO3

— Universo 970 AM (@Universo970py) November 6, 2026

President Santiago Peña, a shareholder in Ueno’s holding company until March 2025, submitted his resignation Thursday, but a joint session of Congress called to accept it adjourned without a quorum, according to La Nación. He then said he would face impeachment—a trial in Congress to remove a president—and defend himself.

The lower house had already voted 78-0 to open impeachment, and on Friday the Senate rejected his request for 72 extra hours to prepare his defense. The Senate planned a removal vote for Friday night, ABC Color reported, with Vice President Pedro Alliana next in line.

Horacio Cartes, the Colorado Party leader and Peña’s former political mentor, withdrew his support and backed the impeachment. “We will not be on the side of the plunder of public, private and retirees’ funds,” Cartes said.

Interior Minister Enrique Riera and central bank president Carlos Carvallo have resigned, and eight Ueno executives have been arrested, according to La Nación’s Friday update. Prosecutors also opened a criminal investigation into Peña’s possible responsibility for the bank’s irregularities, which he denies.

Police used water cannons and tear gas on marchers near Congress, and a new law requires most deposits to be returned within five business days, though protesters have not been placated.

The central bank says its deposit guarantee covers up to 228.3 million guaraníes per person, about $36,000, but as Decrypt previously reported, the first payouts will go only to accounts with up to 10 million guaraníes (around $1,600) and no debts to Ueno.

Bitcoiners, of course, rushed to use Paraguay’s banking crisis as an example on the benefits of decentralization and self custody, and why the “Bitcoin fixes this” motto is still relevant in today’s financial system. “Bitcoin adoption at a nation-state level can mitigate these types of situations,” said JAN3 founder Samson Mow. “If BTC is available to the people as a parallel rail, then systemic banking failures have far less impact.”

The Federation of Educators of Paraguay has called a national strike and march for Monday, Nov. 9, Diario Hoy reported.

#5091. GOPAX halts crypto trading, withdrawals to move all assets to cold storage

South Korea’s GOPAX suspended trading, deposits and standard withdrawals after a hacking threat, with manual withdrawals available through support.

South Korean crypto exchange GOPAX suspended trading, deposits and standard withdrawals Friday after receiving an email threatening a hack, according to local newspaper Kyunghyang Shinmun.

A GOPAX representative told the newspaper that no hack or attempted theft had occurred at that point.

GOPAX said it halted deposits and standard withdrawals at 5:55 am UTC. Trading stopped at 6:53 UTC, according to a second announcement. All unfilled orders were canceled.

GOPAX said it plans to transfer all crypto assets to offline wallets to protect users following a series of leaks and hacking incidents affecting financial institutions. Its notices cited financial regulators’ recommendations.

Cointelegraph reached out to GOPAX for comment but had not received a response by publication time.

Customers can still request withdrawals through GOPAX’s support center, but the exchange warned that manual verification and approvals could delay processing.

The suspension came days after South Korea’s Financial Services Commission (FSC) urged domestic exchanges to increase cold-wallet holdings following the $6.3 billion Binance hack. South Korean exchanges must keep at least 80% of customer crypto assets in cold storage.

Financial Supervisory Service data previously showed that GOPAX held 80.54% of user assets offline at the end of September, near the required minimum.

In October, the FSC reported cybersecurity deficiencies at 130 of 555 financial companies reviewed nationwide. The regulator also identified GOPAX operator Streami and crypto exchange Prabang among 16 companies involved in earlier security incidents, prompting plans for tighter cybersecurity oversight.

South Korean exchange Prabang also suspended services after detecting abnormal withdrawals from its hot wallet on Oct. 24. The exchange reported about 100 million won ($70,000) in losses, according to Yonhap News Agency. Prabang said it would reimburse affected customers using company funds.

#5089. Why a Hyperliquid Treasury Firm Wants to Become a US Futures Broker

Why a Hyperliquid Treasury Firm Wants to Become a US Futures Broker

Hyperliquid Strategies built its business around holding HYPE. Now it wants to help Americans trade on Hyperliquid, too.

The Nasdaq-listed treasury firm, which trades as PURR and is chaired by former Barclays CEO Bob Diamond, disclosed in its quarterly results Thursday that a subsidiary has applied to become a futures commission merchant, or FCM.

That’s the type of CFTC-registered broker that holds customer funds and handles futures orders. If approved, the unit would carry U.S. client accounts for permissioned markets built on Hyperliquid, letting Americans trade perpetual futures within a regulated framework.

Perpetual futures are derivatives with no expiration date that let traders take leveraged positions. Hyperliquid, a major onchain venue for them, still blocks U.S. users.

Its HIP-3 framework lets outside firms launch their own perpetual markets on Hyperliquid’s order book after staking HYPE. A new permissioned version, called HIP-3*, would let a regulated deployer run markets restricted to approved traders, with Hyperliquid acting as neutral infrastructure.

“We deployed $802 million this quarter and filed an application to become a futures commission merchant,” CEO David Schamis said. “Those two moves are the same bet.”

The firm held about 38.5 million HYPE at the end of September, worth roughly $3.5 billion, and more U.S. trading routed through Hyperliquid would likely mean more fees for the protocol, which uses fee revenue to buy back HYPE. An FCM would also give Hyperliquid Strategies a business of its own beyond holding tokens.

During the quarter, the firm spent $785 million buying 9.1 million HYPE and $17 million repurchasing 2.7 million of its own shares, funded largely by $911 million raised through stock sales. It reported $620.6 million in net income, driven almost entirely by $803 million in unrealized gains as HYPE rose from about $65 to $91.

It won’t be alone. Kraken parent Payward has said it plans to deploy HIP-3* markets through its CFTC-regulated Bitnomial exchange, with NinjaTrader Clearing serving as the FCM.

And last week, NYSE owner Intercontinental Exchange agreed to team up with Trade[XYZ] to bring Hyperliquid-based perpetual futures to the U.S., sending HYPE up 13%. Trade[XYZ] already runs markets on Hyperliquid, including perpetual futures tied to the S&P 500.

Regulators must sign off on both the FCM registration and the permissioned HIP-3 markets it would serve, and the company has given no timeline.

Ultimately, if you’re maxing out at $150 opt for this device to enjoy the massaging and hair removal benefits.

If you need razor-sharp precision, this device has the smallest head on the list. It’s designed to quickly, safely, and easily pluck away multiple stray hairs in your eyebrow area. You can still use this on your face, lips, chin, and even nose, but it’ll just take a little longer because the tip is so small. And whether you’ve got a few stray hairs, bushy brows, or even a unibrow, this device has you covered.

We’re impressed that such an affordable device doesn’t cut corners. Instead, it has hypoallergenic stainless steel blades that minimize the risk of redness, irritation, and scratches. Like pricier alternatives, this just under-$9 find also has a built-in LED light to illuminate your eyebrows so you can ensure even the tiniest, lightest strands are found and eliminated. It’s also the most affordable waterproof hair removal device on our list, though only the shaver head is waterproof, so it’s best to use in a dry setting.

This device comes with a USB cord that you can plug into your laptop, along with a single replacement blade that can last between three to six months, depending on usage. Even if you use this tool twice a month, it’s like you’re spending $1.50 per month over the course of half a year. Not too shabby!

Rest easy and reach for this device when you want to use an affordable epilator from a trusted brand that’s great for beginners. Braun is one of the most legit hair removal companies in the game, but don’t just take our word for it. This device earned the most Amazon reviews (a casual 4.2-star average from nearly 22,000) among our expert-vetted recommendations.

So what makes this sub-$40 epilator worth it? Thanks to interchangeable heads, you can epilate, trim, and shave with a single device. You can use the gentle trimmer to cut down longer body hairs, an epilator to remove them from the root, and the shaver attachment to clean up any areas when you’re in a pinch. Swapping out the attachments is seamless—and the shaver is awesome for days I want smooth skin, but my body hair isn't long enough for epilating.

Beginners will especially love the trimmer because it doesn't irritate our sensitive skin. And the epilator head has a built-in massage roller that helps minimize any discomfort. While it's not as effective as our best overall epilator, it gets the job done by removing all of our unwanted body hair in around 30 minutes. Most importantly, the pain is rather minimal. Not too shabby for a compact, travel-friendly pick!

Like our No. 1 pick, this more affordable tool is also equipped with a smartlight feature to illuminate your skin and spotlight inconspicuous body hairs. It’s also equipped with two speed settings, making it helpful for beginners and novices alike.

The biggest downside with this tool is that it’s not designed to remove unwanted facial hair. That said, you can pluck away rogue chin hairs or reach for the Amazon bestselling Kitsch dermaplaning tool that trims your brows and shaves your face to reveal smoother skin.

Epilators are unique because they’re equipped with built-in tweezers to pluck hairs from the root.

The smaller heads have fewer tweezers, but they're fantastic for targeted areas like the face, underarms, and bikini line.

Larger epilator heads are better for covering bigger areas like your legs. Having a lot of tweezers helps speed up your hair removal routine, but it also means you’ll have a more painful experience because it’s plucking dozens of hairs at a time. But like ripping a bandage off a hairy arm, it’s uncomfortable at first, but not too rough. And FWIW, Dr. King promises the pain level noticeably improves after multiple sessions. So be brave and patient, and it’ll all be over before you know it.

For the most effective hair removal, your body hair should be about the length of a sprinkle or grain of rice (about 0.25 inches or 6.35 mm). This standard for epilators is the same as waxing and sugaring. When your hair is too short, it's challenging for the epilator to grasp and remove the strands. You also don't want them to be longer than about two rice grains to help prevent hairs from getting tangled and minimize pain. Everyone's hair regrowth is different, but for most people, stubble typically occurs within one to two weeks.

It sounds like a small detail, but also consider the power source.

If you have minimal outlets in your home, then battery-powered makes sense. However, the hidden cost of replacing batteries can start to add up within the year. That said, all of the featured battery-operated devices in this story include a battery to help offset the long-term cost.

These devices are great because you won’t have to worry about them cranking out mid-epilating, but honestly the cords can get in the way. If you’ve got the extra funds, it’s best to invest in a cordless device so you can remove your unwanted body hair without stretching in odd positions or sitting on your bedroom floor. That said, there are some stellar corded options, including the relatively affordable $40 Philips Epilator Series 2000.

Water-resistant devices can be used wet or dry, so the cordless option can be used in your shower and the corded version works on your damp body if you prefer. Most aren’t waterproof, except the Braun Epilator Silk-épil 9 Flex 9-481 Beauty Set and the epilator head on the Colordiary Eyebrow Hair Remover.

As our beauty editor, I’m constantly on the hunt for the best products, and spend the vast majority of my time testing and reviewing the latest drops. I also regularly consult dermatologists about all things beauty, which is exactly what I did to kick off the selection process for our best epilators story. After talking with Dr. King (she’s one of my most trusted dermatological experts), I asked about her recommendations and insights into shopping for epilators. Then, our team tested several of her picks and compared them to some of the other epilators in our beauty closet.

We've been testing epilators since March 2021, and over time we've evaluated dozens of newer models with various features until we settled on these seven winners. In the years since, we've kept a close eye on the latest launches across all price points and have updated this story at least yearly to share our honest thoughts. For our most recent update, we tried 12 epilators and determined these seven are worthy of a place in our routine—and we're confident they'll equally impress you as well.

Over the last six weeks we've been trying a slew of options and kept the number of tweezers, power source, and water resistance in mind. Next, we paid attention to the number of attachments. We prioritized accessories that helped alleviate discomfort to ensure we have less intimidating options for beginners. Next, we considered each epilator's ease of use, noise level, grip strength, comfort, and efficacy at removing hair. Our team used these devices in our real lives and ahead of important events, like weddings, birthdays, and after exercise.

While yes, the top pick is over $250, it’s from Braun, one of the most-trusted names in the hair removal game. It also earned major snaps from McMahon and me. And FWIW, our other picks are $140 and under, with some in the sweet $10-$40 range.

If you’re interested in giving epilating a shot, the above are some highly reviewed options that will get you on your way to smooth, bare skin.

These devices look like chunky electric razors and are either manual like a traditional women’s razor or powered by batteries or an electrical outlet. When you run the device across your body, tiny tweezers pluck away unwanted body hair. So yes, the initial hair removal process will be a tad uncomfy, but the smooth results are so worth it, says Dr. King.

Like all other hair removal techniques, using an epilator is all about skin prep. Dr. King recommends a gentle physical exfoliation, such as a body scrub, gloves, mitt, loofah, or brush, before epilation. (Psst: I’ve been using Nécessaire The Body Exfoliant since 2019 and haven’t looked back since.) "This will help to expose hairs so they can be picked up by the epilator, and it also helps decrease the risk of ingrown hairs," says Dr. King.

#5087. Greece approves 10% tax on crypto gains starting in 2027

Greece has approved a 10% tax on cryptocurrency gains starting in 2027, with an exemption for investors whose annual net gains do not exceed 500 euros.

Greece’s parliament approved legislation imposing a 10% tax on cryptocurrency gains, establishing new rules for investors as the country prepares to introduce the levy in 2027.

The Hellenic Parliament passed the tax measures Thursday as part of a broader bill covering loan oversight, debt protections and financial market regulation.

The bill introduces a 10% tax on profits from crypto asset sales but exempts investors whose annual gains do not exceed 500 euros ($560), local financial publication Oikonomikos Tachydromos reported Friday. The new framework is scheduled to take effect on Jan. 1, 2027.

The legislation also imposes a 15% tax on income from staking, crypto lending and providing liquidity to decentralized protocols. Using crypto to buy goods or services may also trigger capital gains tax, while swapping one crypto asset for another does not generate taxable gains, the report notes.

Greece’s Ministry of National Economy and Finance first proposed the tax in October. The legislation gives investors 12 months to voluntarily declare previously unreported crypto gains. They must pay the resulting tax within 60 days of filing to avoid penalties and interest.

#5086. Greece Passes Crypto Tax Law With 10% Gains Levy, 15% on Staking and DeFi

Greece Passes Crypto Tax Law With 10% Gains Levy, 15% on Staking and DeFi

Greece's parliament passed a bill Thursday imposing a 10% capital-gains tax on cryptocurrency profits, marking a shift from a proposal discussed in October to legislation approved by lawmakers.

The measure also puts a 15% tax on income from staking, crypto lending and decentralized finance, or DeFi.

The legislation’s crypto provisions sit inside a wider Finance Ministry package dealing with debt servicing, financial oversight, and government accounting, with the new crypto income-tax rules scheduled for January 1, 2027.

Individual investors with annual net crypto gains of €500 ($557) or less will be exempt. Cross that threshold, though, and the 10% tax applies to the entire gain, rather than just the amount over €500 ($557).

For example, an investor with €600 ($669) in net gains would face a €60 ($67) tax under the new rate, not €10 ($11). The rules generally calculate gains by subtracting the acquisition cost from the value received when the crypto is disposed of.

Selling Bitcoin for euros is one taxable event, but spending crypto on goods or services can also count as a disposal. Investors will need transaction records to establish what they paid for their assets and what those assets were worth when sold or spent.

Yield from staking, lending, and providing liquidity to DeFi protocols is treated differently. Greece will classify those returns as interest income and tax them at 15%, according to the Finance Ministry.

Qualifying crypto losses exceeding €500 ($557) can be carried forward for five years and offset against future crypto gains. The bill also gives investors a 12-month window to voluntarily declare previously unreported crypto income without late-filing penalties or interest, provided they meet its conditions and pay within 60 days of filing.

The passage follows an October public consultation on the proposal.The draft proposed a 10% rate that was already lower than the 15% levy Greek officials had discussed in June.

Athens says exchanging one cryptocurrency for another should not itself trigger a capital-gains tax. But the wording lawmakers approved may not make that exclusion as clear as the government intended.

A review by parliament's Scientific Service flagged the bill's broad language covering disposals made "for consideration." That phrase could include token-to-token trades, the review said, even though the accompanying explanation says such swaps should not create taxable gains.

The reviewers recommended an explicit exemption and suggested clearer rules for exchanges involving stablecoins and electronic-money tokens. Those details matter for traders who rotate between assets without cashing out to fiat currency.

Greece is also preparing the administrative rules needed to put the tax into practice. A senior Finance Ministry official told Reuters that guidance on transaction transparency and tax audits would be issued in the coming weeks.

The government has not estimated how much the tax will raise. Officials say the market is difficult to measure because many Greek crypto investors use trading platforms based overseas.

#5084. The 6 Best Undereye Patches, Tested and Reviewed by Dermatologists and Beauty Editors

The entire Women's Health team swears by them.

Women's Health may earn commission from the links on this page, but we only feature products we believe in. Why Trust Us?

I’m always up late doomscrolling TikTok and The White Lotus subreddit for season 4 theories. (Natasha Rothwell hive, stand up!) The only problem? Those late nights mean I have dark circles and puffiness in the morning (boo!). While I'm a huge fan of undereye creams, sometimes I get bored and need to switch things up. Enter undereye patches. They're a fast fix for dryness, puffiness, and dark circles—and even help me fake eight hours of sleep. "The skin under the eyes is prone to changes related to aging skin, such as fine lines, and conditions like dryness and discoloration," says dermatologist Brendan Camp, MD. In just 10 to 20 minutes, undereye patches temporarily hydrate the delicate skin and help me look refreshed. Plus, they're super cute and add a fun touch to my selfies, even if they're shared to just my Close Friends circle on Instagram Stories.

Like any other skin care product, eye masks really get their power from their ingredients, some of which are typically hyaluronic acid, vitamin C, niacinamide, and caffeine.

“Eye patches incorporate key ingredients like hyaluronic acid to boost moisture, peptides to firm, ceramides to strengthen the skin barrier, caffeine to help reduce puffiness, and niacinamide to reduce discoloration,” says dermatologist Marisa Garshick, MD.

As with any beauty product, there are always a few things to consider before making the investment. Since these masks are mostly advertised for their ability to soothe and hydrate, you'll want to check the hydrating and firming ingredients, and keep your expectations realistic since results are usually temporary. Here's what to look out for.

For starters, look for key ingredients such as aloe, chamomile, cucumber, green tea, glycerin, or hyaluronic acid. You're less likely to come across vitamin C and retinol, but it's a plus if you do. Patches with a cellulose gel material can also prevent them from sliding off your face (a con I often deal with and try to avoid as much as possible).

Not only is the skin under your eyes thinner and more delicate than in other areas of your face, but it’s also where lymphatic fluid drainage occurs, says dermatologist Lauren Penzi, MD. It can also show more blood vessels, and in turn, make dark circles appear more visible, says Dr. Garshick. Most eye masks are created to reduce fine lines, undereye darkness, and wrinkles while giving your skin the boost of moisture it needs. The overall effect of these patches is to revive and hydrate, which is why so many people use them to get a more refreshed look.

Many people complain about looking tired, and eye patches are a nice, quick fix. Still, it's important to be realistic about the kind of results you'll get in just one or two uses. So, you’ll want to zero in on the reason you’re buying patches, says Dr. Camp. For example, you can target puffiness by rocking undereye patches infused with caffeine or green tea while getting ready for work, says Dr. Camp.

As a member of the beauty team at Women’s Health, I’m constantly on the hunt for the best products and spend a lot of my time testing and reviewing the latest drops. I frequently consult skin experts, which is exactly what I did to kick off my selection process here. I consulted five New York-based dermatologists and had them give their recommendations with insight on why these undereye masks are so splurge-worthy. Then, I tested several of their picks and compared them to some of the other undereye masks available in our beauty closet.To narrow down the list even further, I got a few other members of team WH, including Addison Aloian, Lily Wohlner, Brian Underwood, and Danielle Jackson, to get in on the testing process. Ingredients, price, and overall efficacy were taken into account to determine which would be best for a wide range of needs and preferences.Here are the fruits of our labor.

After testing tons of undereye masks, this one takes the cake for best overall. Why? It's the most hydrating option on this list and it doesn't slip off your face, which is super hard to find in an undereye mask.

ICYMI, Wander Beauty basically started the undereye mask trend. This OG mask is made with peptides, amino acids, and hyaluronic acid to improve your skin's texture, tone, and hydration. It's also expert-approved by Dr. Garshick and dermatologist Dendy Engelman, MD. "I love that this mask is made with three different layers that allow the formula to penetrate the skin deeply and prevent it from sliding off the face, so you can multitask while you wear it," says Dr. Engelman.

Wander Beauty patches are as effective as they are Instagrammable. I’ve been a fan of the rose gold and yellow gold patches for five years, and all of my beauty editor friends gush over them. They brighten, hydrate, and plump up my fine lines. They also contain calendula extract and aloe vera to help reduce puffiness. Ahem, hi holiday parties and upcoming travel—I’m looking at you.

These pretty patches have three unique layers: the surface's foil retains heat and prevents evaporation, the middle grips to prevent slippage, and the base delivers moisturizing glycerin and depuffing calendula extract. For an extra soothing experience, I like to leave mine in the fridge. Cool temperatures help shrink blood vessels under the eyes, so dark circles appear lighter and puffiness minimizes, dermatologist Marisa Garshick, MD, said in our guide to undereye skin care.

That said, the construction of this mask consists of super light and flexible material versus the conventional plastic material found in a lot of other eye patches that were tested, giving this product a high-quality feel.

I would be remiss if I didn't mention my personal fave patch from Patchology. It contains hyaluronic acid (hi, hydrated, plump skin) and feels super cool under the eyes to de-puff and smooth out that area.

Patchology is fantastic for targeting lines, says dermatologist Anar Mikailov, MD. He mentions that there's resveratrol, a natural antioxidant found in grapes that helps improve barrier function, and other antioxidants that provide protection against oxidizing agents that can visibly age the skin by causing wrinkles. There’s also antioxidant-rich strawberry extract, which helps brighten dark circles.

After using this patch, I also noticed my undereye area was less puffy and more luminous. My only con was that the mask slides out of place after a while, but it’s still epic to use and totally worth incorporating into your routine. My hack is to use these patches while lying down in bed to prevent any slipping.

This undereye mask is basically an expert at reducing puffy, tired-looking eyes thanks to its cooling effects. It also temporarily plumps up my fine lines with hydrating hyaluronic acid.

This option comes with a lot of serum, so it may slip down your face over time. But, feel free to go ahead and massage that extra serum into your undereye area—that little bit of massaging can help assist with lymphatic drainage and further reduce puffiness. Need even more convincing? Celebs like Jessica Alba post about this mask on her Instagram story time and time again, noting that she's a major fan.

Most masks on this list only need 10 minutes on the skin, but this one should stay on for up to 20. Although, 20 minutes fly by when you have a good book in hand (pro tip!).

These eye masks from Live Tinted are packed with copper peptides and banana extract (hi, Ashton Hall!) to reduce puffiness and smooth fine lines. They also reduce puffiness after just a few uses and leave skin looking glowy and feeling hydrated.

And because these patches help soften and smooth your delicate undereye area, Aloian relies on them for makeup prep. "Not only is the patch super cute—it's shaped like a sun—but it also genuinely hydrates and brightens my undereye area," she says. These patches make Aloian feel confident going makeup free because of the glow they leave behind, but it's just as helpful on days when she wants a dewy base before applying concealer.

Along with a mix of peptides to help stimulate collagen production, this eye mask contains niacinamide, bakuchiol, caffeine, and vitamin C. Bakuchiol is a great retinol alternative that helps stimulate collagen production, says Dr. Mikailov.

Wohlner also gave this patch a try and loves how flexible it is under the eye. However, she did note that it does tend to slip down the face because it's super thin.

This vitamin C-packed eye patch is perfect for helping brighten up the undereye area while reducing signs of aging and puffiness with the help of caffeine.

Yeah, it's a little pricier, but trust that it's totally worth it if you have the budget. The gel patches are super easy to apply. Plus, they can reduce discoloration and fine lines within just a few weeks. Sometimes I'm wary of products with vitamin C in them because the smell can be a bit aggressive, but these are just right.

Derms also deem this patch worth the money. "The key ingredient in this mask is THD Ascorbate Vitamin C, which is 50 times more powerful than other forms of vitamin C," says Dr. Engelman. THD is especially good for surface dark spots that need brightening rather than deeper dark circles, says Dr. Mikailov.

It's worth noting that these dry up around the 10-minute mark, and while they stay on really well, the edges break off easily if you try to move the masks around a few minutes after the initial application.

As the name suggests, this patch is meant to soothe tired-looking eyes. So, for those of you who have a lot of sleepless nights or are just night owls at heart, this is your winner. Infused with caffeine, niacinamide, and hyaluronic acid, these work overtime to reduce puffiness and dark circles.

"I often recommend Peace Out’s acne patch for the face, but I also love the brand’s eye patches," says Dr. Engelman. "It's so effective at quickly bringing down puffiness and brightening and smoothing the under-eyes." The patch is biodegradable and made with biocellulose, which helps the formula deeply penetrate the skin, says Dr. Engelman.

These patches fit snugly under my eyes, so I could wear them while doing my eye makeup. They also did a great job of catching eyeshadow fallout that would normally ruin my undereye concealer. Another pro? Unlike most gel patches, they weren’t completely soaked in serum, which can make the process a bit messy. Instead, they had just the right amount for ample hydration. After the initial use, I noticed my undereyes were softer and brighter, and I’d slowly reach for these patches for special occasions.

I’m not the only fan on WH’s beauty team. “I have very sensitive skin, so I'm always cautious when it comes to trying things out, but this didn't give me any issues,” says tester Sabrina Talbert. “It also doesn't have much of a scent, which put my mind at ease since fragrance tends to be an early indicator that my skin might not like a product.”

These patches are on the larger side, so they feel a bit bulky. They also need to be slightly adjusted because they slide down a little bit over 15 minutes, but honestly, they’re such minor cons that Talbert continues using them.

#5082. Treasury Cuts Crypto Exchange MEXC Off From US Financial System

Treasury Cuts Crypto Exchange MEXC Off From US Financial System

The U.S. Treasury Department has moved to sever crypto exchange MEXC Global from the American financial system, accusing it of facilitating at least $16 billion in illicit transactions tied to Iran, North Korea and Russia.

In an order issued Friday through its Financial Crimes Enforcement Network, Treasury said MEXC, one of the world’s 20 largest crypto exchanges, served as a major conduit for sanctions evasion and cybercrime.

The action, part of a broader effort dubbed Operation Economic Outcast, bars U.S. financial institutions from processing any funds transfers to or from the exchange. It takes effect 30 days after publication in the Federal Register.

“Financial institutions cannot have it both ways: Either you support the Iranian regime, or you have access to the U.S. financial system,” Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent said in a statement, vowing to keep severing the regime’s “economic lifelines” and its attempts to use digital assets to dodge sanctions.

Founded in 2018, MEXC processes more than $1 billion in daily transactions, holds around $5 billion in reserves, and offers more than 3,000 tokens, according to FinCEN. Between May 2021 and May 2026, FinCEN said, the platform moved at least $16 billion connected to illicit activity, including nearly $1.5 billion tied to Russian sanctions evasion, with $232 million in direct transactions with sanctioned entities. The agency named MEXC a “primary money laundering concern” in connection with Russian illicit finance.

Despite publicly claiming anti-money-laundering controls and barring users in places like Iran and North Korea, MEXC’s compliance was riddled with “severe deficiencies,” FinCEN said.

The exchange was still transacting with sanctioned Russian exchange Garantex and its successor Grinex as recently as June 2026, more than four years after Garantex was first designated. It also processed roughly $200 million linked to the A7A5 stablecoin and about $22 million benefiting Iran’s central bank and its Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps.

Those connections place MEXC squarely within a sprawling sanctions-evasion ecosystem that Treasury has been dismantling piece by piece.

The department last month designated Russia’s A7 Network a transnational criminal organization and proposed cutting its front companies off from U.S. finance, flagging more than $179 billion in transactions through its ruble-backed A7A5 token, much of it routed through Garantex and Grinex.

The same campaign has reached into Iran’s crypto sector, where Treasury gained sweeping authority to sanction anyone operating in it and blacklisted the Iranian exchange Nobitex over its role in funneling crypto to the IRGC.

The order uses a provision of the Combating Russian Money Laundering Act rather than a standard sanctions designation, a tool FinCEN has increasingly reached for to cut off crypto infrastructure that operates outside the correspondent banking system.

#5079. Binance hackers had 8-week intrusion window before $6.3B exploit: SEAL

The cybersecurity company flagged similar multi-stage intrusion patterns behind other high-profile crypto heists, including the $388 million Bitget hack.

Updated (Nov. 6 at 05:03 p.m. UTC): This article has been corrected to clarify the timeline of the Binance hack. The article also includes a statement from a Binance spokesperson.

Nonprofit cybersecurity organization Security Alliance (SEAL) estimates that North Korea-linked hackers compromised a Binance employee’s device about eight weeks before the exchange’s $6.3 billion exploit on Oct. 29, although Binance said the broader attack campaign began in June and that the hackers did not gain access to additional wallet infrastructure devices until Oct. 20.

The hacking group compromised a Binance software engineer’s device in September through a fake recruitment scheme, wrote SEAL in a Thursday report, adding that threat actors can navigate compromised systems with a “high degree of stealth over prolonged periods of time” after initial intrusion. SEAL’s eight-week estimate refers to the September-to-October intrusion window, rather than the beginning of the broader campaign described by Binance.

Cointelegraph contacted Binance for clarification on the timeline. A spokesperson said the initial device compromises occurred in June and September, but that attackers did not gain the access needed to reach wallet operations until Oct. 20.

“As outlined in our announcement, the initial device compromises occurred in June and September, but the attackers did not gain the access needed to reach wallet operations until October 20, when they used that foothold to move laterally and obtain access to additional devices and credentials,” the spokesperson said.

SEAL identified at least four “front companies” associated with TraderTraitor’s fake recruitment campaigns in 2026, including citalab.net, citalab.com, bitfit.io and oddium.io.

Binance traced the attack campaign back to June, when a separate employee’s device was compromised after the employee downloaded a tampered Homebrew installation while attempting to install an AI coding tool. The exchange detected the incident and replaced the device, rotating related credentials. However, the attackers had already obtained information about employees working on Binance’s wallet infrastructure, according to a Tuesday X post.

On Oct. 20, attackers used the compromised computer of the engineer targeted in September and an internal AI tool to gain control of two wallet infrastructure engineers’ devices, according to Binance. They subsequently discovered a deployment permission token, which was used during the Oct. 29 exploit.

The cybersecurity organization’s analysis pointed to similar multi-stage intrusion patterns in other high-profile crypto hacks. It estimated that attackers had about six weeks of access before the $388 million Bitget hack, six weeks of access to LayerZero before the vulnerability that led to the $293 million Kelp DAO exploit and about three weeks before conducting the $24 million AFX exploit.

The US Federal Bureau of Investigation attributed the attack to North Korea’s TraderTraitor, in a Saturday report. Kim Yo Jong, sister of North Korean leader Kim Jong Un, dismissed the accusations.

#5078. Sony Axing Game Discs Will Have Outsized Impact on Low-Income Gamers, Experts Say

Sony Axing Game Discs Will Have Outsized Impact on Low-Income Gamers, Experts Say

Gamers in low-income areas will be among the hardest hit by Sony’s decision to stop producing physical game discs from 2028, experts told Decrypt, while one African games industry expert arguing the continent stands to lose out badly.

The secondary market has long been a cheap way for gamers to get hold of games. The PlayStation Store does run sales, but buying digitally assumes a decent internet connection, which rural households are less likely to have.

"By abandoning physical discs, some players at the bottom of the economic spectrum lose access to physical distribution through retail and, more importantly, discounted used sales," Joost van Dreunen, professor at the NYU Stern School of Business and video game business author, told Decrypt. "It won't immediately show up in the financials of major game makers, since their focus is now on creating premium experiences for a luxury class of gamers."

It comes as the cost of being a gamer is trending upwards. Sony raised PS5 prices in March, for example, citing "pressures in the global economic landscape." Similarly, the next generation of Xbox is expected to cost up to $1,800, according to Fortune.

A spokesperson for UK advocacy group Gamer's Voice told Decrypt that Sony's move to cut discs will "likely affect people on lower incomes more."

Isa Muhammad, founder of Africa and Middle East-focused games industry platform Bijetic, told Decrypt that the PS5 is already "out of reach for most Africans." He stressed that those who can buy a console still rely on the second-hand games market to afford to play on the consoles.

Well, at least the highest-income Africans could still take advantage of the PlayStation Store discounts, right? Unfortunately not. Muhammad said that the PlayStation Network is only supported in one African nation: South Africa. Many other nations are missing from the PlayStation Network's official list of supported countries.

"A gamer in Nigeria who wants to buy digitally has to set up an account registered in the U.S. or UK, then pay with foreign gift cards or a dollar card," Muhammad said.

Download speeds in rural, remote, and low-income areas are another major hurdle.

"Bandwidth is now part of what a game costs," van Dreunen said. "Roughly one in four rural [American] households has no wired provider offering 100/20 Mbps, and at 10 Mbps a 50GB download takes about 11 hours."

In Africa and the Middle East, Muhammad said, internet speeds vary significantly across the region—from super-fast connections in Dubai to unreliable ones in Iran. Internet access is also costly in some countries.

"Just recently I paid 75,000 NGN (around $55) for a 450GB broadband bundle, which is more than Nigeria's minimum monthly wage," Muhammad told Decrypt. With GTA 6's pre-load file expected to be 212GB, "a gamer here would first spend $80 on the game, then anywhere from $25 to over $60 just to download it, depending on how they buy data."

And at Nigeria's median download speed of 36.77 Mbps, per Speedtest by Ookla, the download would take nearly 13 hours. Not to mention the fact that they'd need to have a non-Nigerian PlayStation account, a download code corresponding to the country their account is supposedly from, and a way to pay in the local currency.

"Sony's own terms say you must register your account in the country where you live, and Sony can suspend or close accounts created with false information," Muhammad told Decrypt. "So every Nigerian or Egyptian on a US or UK PSN account is breaking the rules from day one."

African gamers live in fear of needing help from Sony and being refused it (or worse, banned) because their accounts break its rules, Muhammad explained. In a future where every purchase is digital, it could open the door to gamers losing accounts that hold every game they've ever purchased.

"If a player in Africa buys GTA 6 and can't recover their account, they may have no choice but to buy it again on a new one, along with everything else they've lost," he said. "A disc-free future will see this become the reality for every new PlayStation game, for gamers across most of Africa."

Sony and PlayStation did not respond to Decrypt's request for comment.

#5076. What You Should Know About Exercising When Bone Density Is a Concern

Osteopenia or osteoporosis doesn’t have to sideline your workouts. Here’s what to do, how to modify, and when to back off.

You might have heard that being active is a good idea when you have osteopenia or osteoporosis, conditions marked by lower-than-normal bone density. But when you’re exercising specifically to improve bone health, some types of exercise may be more helpful than others. Before lacing up your sneakers, there are a few things to know.

“When patients ask me what they can do to preserve and protect their bone health, consistent weight bearing and resistance exercises are the first things I recommend,” says Nicole Leung, MD, director of the Osteoporosis and Bone Health Program at NYU Langone Health in New York City. “Bone is a living tissue, so it adapts to mechanical load through remodeling.”

When bone tissue is stressed via physical activity, your body responds by making more bone, helping strengthen the tissue. Exercise also lowers the activity of cells that dissolve and break down old bone. That combo helps preserve or even improve your bone mineral density, regardless of your health status. Exercise may slow or even prevent the progression of osteoporosis; if you have osteoporosis already, it can help you maintain the bone you have and reduce your risk for falls and fractures.

A bone-density diagnosis doesn’t mean you have to give up exercise—but your routine should reflect your fracture risk and health history. Here’s what Dr. Leung recommends for choosing activities, adapting your routine, and knowing when to check with your doctor before making major changes.

There are a few types of activities that make up a bone-friendly exercise plan, and it’s important to get the right amount of each. Here are the types of exercise to do regularly.

These are exercises where you’re on your feet and putting force on your muscles and bones as they work against gravity. Depending on factors like your age, fitness level, and bone mineral density, these activities can include:

People working to maintain their bone health should do these sorts of activities for 30 to 60 minutes, three to five days per week, according to the American College of Sports Medicine.

As for activities like swimming and cycling, they’re great for getting your heart pumping—but because they’re low-impact, they won’t do much for your bones. That said, feel free to do them in addition to your weight-bearing exercises if you enjoy them.

Lifting (relatively) heavy things works your muscles and helps build bone density. This includes free weights, machines, resistance bands, or body weight exercises like squats or push-ups. Aim for two to three strength sessions per week, and don’t be afraid to gradually go heavier (with your doctor’s okay). Pushing yourself to 70 to 90 percent of your max effort may deliver a bigger bone boost than lifting lighter weights, research suggests.

Having osteoporosis makes you more prone to fractures after falls, but you can strengthen your balance—and reduce your chances for a broken bone—with exercises like yoga, tai chi, or Pilates. “Any exercise where you have to shift your body weight side to side and forward and back can be very helpful for your stabilizing muscles,” Dr. Leung says. She recommends working balance exercises into your schedule a few times per week. Find safe modifications if you’re worried about falling, like holding onto a sturdy chair or touching a wall.

Whether you’ve been exercising for years, are just starting out, or are returning to a routine after a long hiatus, Dr. Leung advises paying attention to your posture, avoiding exercise when you’re overly exhausted, and increasing intensity gradually. Older women who are new to exercise can still do resistance training, she says, but may need to start at a slower pace and under supervision.

Despite the overall benefits of exercise, you still need to be careful, particularly when you have osteoporosis. Certain activities can set the stage for potential spine fractures, so before starting an exercise program, decide with your doctor or other qualified professional what is and isn’t safe for you.

According to the International Osteoporosis Foundation, you may need to limit or avoid moves that involve:

Yoga, while helpful for balance, can involve poses that put your spine at risk (like forward bends, deep twists, plow pose, shoulder stand, and pigeon pose). If you decide to give it a try, choose a class where the teacher can offer safe modifications, Dr. Leung says. It’s also best to steer clear of activities that come with a high risk of falling from above standing height, like skiing or horseback riding, she adds.

When it comes to stressing your bones, it’s possible to get too much of a good thing. “Overburdening your bone can actually lead to stress fractures,” Dr. Leung says. If you do prolonged intense weight-bearing and high-impact exercises like jogging or jumping rope, avoid scheduling them on back-to-back days. Alternate those workouts with activities like strength training, or have an active recovery day where you walk or do yoga or tai chi, she recommends.

Make it a point to stay active even after you’ve completed the day’s workout. According to Dr. Leung, non-exercise activities like casual walking or doing chores may help to maintain bone density when compared to sedentary behavior. Stroll over to chat with a coworker at their desk during a break, take the stairs when you can, or walk while you call a friend instead of sitting. A few minutes here and there can really add up.

Any new or worsening bone or spine pain is a warning sign to stop exercising and contact your doctor. You should also call your doctor if you fall while exercising, notice that you’ve had height loss, or if your spine appears curved, which could be signs of a spinal fracture. And always stop exercising and seek medical attention if you experience other severe symptoms like dizziness, faintness, or chest pain.

Consistency counts. People with low bone density who stay active over several months tend to see the greatest bone-health benefits, studies show. Choose activities you enjoy, build up gradually, and find a rhythm you can sustain. The goal isn’t to nail every workout—it’s to keep movement in your life.

#5075. The Man Behind Fashion’s Most Dazzling Spectacles Turns His Attention to the Art of Light

For as long as he can remember, Alexandre de Betak has been entranced by light. As a 10-year-old, he rewired his Paris bedroom so that visitors had to be buzzed in, greeted by shifting colored lights. He went on to found Bureau Betak, a set design agency that transformed the runway with its megawatt spectacles for clients like Dior, Gucci, Jacquemus, and Fendi. De Betak estimates that he produced “thousands” of fashion shows—including Saint Laurent’s spring-summer 2020 ready-to-wear extravaganza, for which he bathed the Eiffel Tower in golden light, and installations for Rodarte featuring fluorescent and neon tubes.

A new truss light sculpture by Alexandre de Betak, seen here in a 17th-century Swedish castle

Now, de Betak, who sold his company in 2021, is turning the spotlight on his own creative practice. On November 5, he opened his first solo exhibition of artworks at Salon 94 in New York. The electronically programmed Truss Light Sculptures are inspired by the scaffolding and fixtures of his early runway shows and in some cases literally made from them. “That equipment is old-fashioned now, but I wanted to honor it,” de Betak says. “And I liked the irony that I spent so much energy over the years trying to hide it.”

On view at Salon 94’s Upper East Side town house through December 19, the exhibit presents two series. In the gallery’s contemporary space, the first is composed of freestanding and wall-mounted aluminum structures fitted with LED bulbs. The upstairs Stone Room, a historic space with Caen stone plaster walls and arched windows, is the backdrop for a second series of versions in bronze. To make them, de Betak had a French foundry recast each part down to the smallest screw by hand, a process that sometimes required more than 20 molds.

A pair of wall-mounted sculptures in aluminum

The light sculptures, which vary from sconce-like creations to hulking constructions out of a sci-fi movie, have a sly sense of humor and a human quality. This is especially true when they come to life, thanks to a remote-control-operated system that activates light sequences that glow, sparkle, thrum, or react to ambient noise, “whether a whisper,” de Betak says, “or extremely loud music.” For these effects, he drew on his stage work, where he was renowned for cueing, or choreographing, light and sound. “I’m interested in the ephemeral nature of what this equipment was made to show—whether a fashion show or a ballet,” he says. “At a moment when everything is virtual, I think it’s more important than ever to create things that produce a live emotion.”

Salon 94’s founder Jeanne Greenberg Rohatyn observes that de Betak’s sculptures are equally powerful switched on or off. Unlit they remind her of David Smith artworks or Russian constructivism. Lit, and with synced sound, they evoke the theatricality of stage performers. “Alex has spent his career building structures that disappear into someone else’s spectacle,” she says. “Here, the structure is the whole point.”

#5074. Ethereum devs won’t fork Glamsterdam on Hoodi until smart contract risks resolved

Ethereum developers are investigating concerns tied to eight smart contract invariants tied to Glamsterdam’s EIP-8037 and EIP-8038.

Ethereum core developers delayed the Glamsterdam upgrade’s implementation on the Hoodi testnet until security concerns tied to eight smart contract invariants are resolved.

Some existing Ethereum projects rely on assumptions that certain operations would cost a specific amount of gas, which would be invalidated by Glamsterdam’s EIP-8037 and EIP-8038 because they change how gas is charged for certain operations, the Ethereum Foundation said in a Friday blog post.

The decision followed an Oct. 13 report from smart contract auditor ChainSecurity, which found that the two Ethereum Improvement Proposals (EIPs) could break smart contracts that rely on fixed gas-related assumptions when external calls fail.

Delaying the Hoodi testnet fork gives developers more time to identify potential smart contract issues and determine whether changes are needed before Glamsterdam progresses toward mainnet deployment. The Hoodi fork will not proceed until the issues are resolved, added the Ethereum Foundation’s post.

Ethereum’s Glamsterdam upgrade went live on the Sepolia testnet on Oct. 6, in one of its final major rehearsals. The upgrade aims to advance Ethereum’s layer-1 scaling roadmap with enshrined proposer-builder separation, block-level access lists and changes to gas pricing that better reflect the cost of execution, the Ethereum Foundation announced in a Sept. 28 blog post.
